Diagonal flow detects topology of strata Thanks: This work is in the public domain.
Mark Bell, Vincent Delecroix, Vaibhav Gadre, Rodolfo Gutiérrez-Romo, Saul Schleimer

Abstract
. We study the interplay between the diagonal flow on, and the topology of, a stratum component of a space of rooted quadratic differentials. We prove that the flow group – the subgroup of the fundamental group generated by almost-flow loops – equals the fundamental group. As a corollary, we show that the plus and minus modular Rauzy–Veech groups are finite-index subgroups of their ambient modular monodromy groups. This partially answers a question of Yoccoz. Using this, and recent advances on algebraic hulls and Zariski closures of symplectic monodromy groups, we prove that the Rauzy–Veech groups are Zariski dense in their ambient symplectic groups. Density, in turn, implies the simplicity of the plus and minus Lyapunov spectra of any component of any stratum of quadratic differentials. We thus establish the Kontsevich–Zorich conjecture.



\fail

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 30F30; Secondary 37D40, 32G15, 30F60, 37A20, 37F34, 57K20

1.
Introduction

Motivation

A Riemann surface
S
equipped with an (abelian or quadratic) differential
q
has a canonical flat metric.
This metric determines, and is determined by, the
periods
of the differential:
its integrals along appropriately chosen relative cycles.
Varying
q
, without changing the underlying combinatorics, gives rise to an ambient
stratum component

\mathcal{C}
: a moduli space of differentials.
(The possible components are completely classified; see
[
KZ03
,
Lan08
,
CM14
]
.)
The periods endow the component with (orbifold) charts known as
period coordinates
.
The usual identification of
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathbb{R}^{2}
gives an
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
–action on the period coordinates.

As a hint of deep importance of these spaces, and this action, suppose that
q
is a differential and
\mathcal{N}
is its
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
–orbit closure.
Then
\mathcal{N}
is dynamically defined, yet is an algebraic variety
[
Fil16
]
.
Futhermore, in period coordinates
\mathcal{N}
is cut out by homogeneous linear equations, with (real) algebraic coefficients
[
EMM15
]
.
So, despite the essentially transcendental dependence of flat surfaces on their differentials, their dynamics surprisingly behave much like a Lie group acting on a homogeneous space.

Monodromy

Our goal is to understand the
monodromy
associated to the component
\mathcal{C}
.
There is a forgetful map from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{M}(S)
:
the moduli space of Riemann surface structures on
S
.
Both
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{M}(S)
are orbifolds;
both have various manifold covers.

The (orbifold) universal cover of
\mathcal{M}(S)
is the so-called Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}(S)
;
this is homeomorphic to an open ball in
\mathbb{R}^{6g-6}
where
g=\genus(S)
.
The deck group is the mapping class group
\mathrm{Mod}(S)
.

To obtain finite manifold cover of
\mathcal{C}
we consider
rooted
differentials.
A
root
for
q
is a horizontal unit tangent vector at a singularity.
The choice of root breaks all symmetries of
q

and so unwraps the orbifold locus.
The resulting finite cover is a manifold.
We fix a connected component of this space and denote it by
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.

The above maps of spaces induce the following sequence of homomorphisms:

\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}})\to\uppi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(\mathcal{C})\to\uppi_{1}^{\mathrm{orb}}(\mathcal{M}(S))\cong\mathrm{Mod}(S)\xrightarrow{\rho}\Aut(\mathrm{H}_{1}(S;\mathbb{Z}))\cong\Sp(2g,\mathbb{Z})

The third map,
\rho
, is the symplectic representation of the mapping class group.
We call the image of
\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}})
, inside the mapping class group, the
modular monodromy group
.
The image of the modular monodromy group under
\rho
is known as the
symplectic monodromy group
.

Monodromy groups are “topological offspring” of the stratum component
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
In order to relate the topology and dynamics of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
we ask the following.

Question 1.1
.

Is
\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}})
“detected” by the diagonal flow?
∎

To make this precise, suppose that
U\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
is a small contractible open set.
Suppose that
q_{0}\in U
is a base-point.
Consider the diagonal flow trajectories that start and end in
U
.
For each, we connect its endpoints to
q_{0}
inside of
U
to get a loop based at
q_{0}
.
As
U
is contractible, the resulting based homotopy class is independent of the choices made inside of
U
.
We call these based homotopy classes
almost-flow loops
.
The
flow group
of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
associated with the pair
(U,q_{0})
is the subgroup of
\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}},q_{0})
generated by all such loops.
(A semigroup version, and some of its applications, are discussed by Hamenstädt
[
Ham18
, Section 4.2]
.)
So,
1.1
can be restated as follows.

Question 1.2
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
is a stratum component of the moduli space of (rooted) abelian or quadratic differentials.
Is the flow group of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
equal to
\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}})
?
∎

This is a version of a “question” of Yoccoz
[
Yoc10
, Remark 9.3]
.
1
1

1

Yoccoz asks if the fundamental group of the Rauzy diagram surjects
\mathrm{Mod}(S)
.
However, what is
meant
is the monodromy of
\mathcal{C}
inside of the “obvious” subgroup of
\mathrm{Mod}(S)

[
Mat21
]
.

One of our main results is a positive answer to
1.2
, in the setting of rooted differentials.

.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
is any component of a stratum of the moduli space of rooted abelian or quadratic differentials.
Suppose that
U
is any contractible open set and
q_{0}\in U
is a choice of base-point.
Then the flow group of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
, associated with the pair
(U,q_{0})
, equals
\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}},q_{0})
.

We also define the
strict flow group
associated with the pair
(U,q_{0})
.
This is the subgroup of the flow group of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
generated by those elements which are
closed
diagonal flow trajectories.
We then directly show that the strict flow group and the flow group of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
coincide.
This implies the following, suggested by Forni:

.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
is any component of a stratum of the moduli space of rooted abelian or quadratic differentials.
Suppose that
U
is any contractible open set and
q_{0}\in U
is a choice of base-point.
Then the strict flow group of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
, associated with the pair
(U,q_{0})
, equals
\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}},q_{0})
.

Through the zippered rectangles construction, the diagonal flow on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
can be coded combinatorially by a Rauzy diagram.
In the course of the proof of
Theorem

5.9
, we prove the following.

.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
be the Rauzy diagram for
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
Then the natural homomorphism
\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}})\to\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}})
is surjective.

For strata of abelian differentials, previous work by Calderon and Calderon–Salter also allows us to explicitly compute the image of the flow group inside of
\mathrm{Mod}(S)
and of
\Aut(\mathrm{H}_{1}(S;\mathbb{Z}))
(or some larger group, such as
\Aut(\mathrm{H}_{1}(S,Z;\mathbb{Z}))
), up to finite index
[
Cal20
,
CS21
,
CS21a
,
CS23
]
.

Cocycles

Fix
\mathcal{C}
, a stratum component.
Given a bundle over
\mathcal{C}
, the diagonal flow gives us a natural cocycle.
The most studied of these is the
Kontsevich–Zorich cocycle
.
This can be lifted to a connected component
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{C}
of the Teichmüller space of abelian or quadratic differentials (the choice of
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{C}
is, in general, not unique
[
Cal20
,
CS23
]
).

In more detail, we define a vector bundle over
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{C}
with a suitable fibre.
In the abelian case, this fibre is the first cohomology of the underlying topological surface;
in the quadratic case, it is the first cohomology of the orientation double cover.
By Poincaré-duality, it is also possible to use the corresponding homology groups as the fibre.

The
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
–action induces a trivial dynamical cocycle on this vector bundle.
By modding out by the mapping class group, the vector bundle descends to a bundle over
\mathcal{C}
known as the
Hodge bundle
;
similarly the cocycle descends to the
Kontsevich–Zorich cocycle

[
KZ97
,
Kon97
]
.
In the quadratic case, the cocycle naturally splits into two distinct symplectically orthogonal blocks, usually referred to as the
plus
(or
invariant
) and
minus
(or
anti-invariant
) pieces.

Moduli spaces of abelian or quadratic differentials carry a natural
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
–invariant measure called the Masur–Smillie–Veech measure.
The cocycle is measurable and log-integrable; by Oseledets’ theorem, it has Lyapunov exponents.

Many interesting dynamical properties of abelian or quadratic differentials can be written in terms of the Lyapunov exponents of the Kontsevich–Zorich cocycle.
An important example are the deviations of ergodic averages of the linear flow on almost every abelian or quadratic differential
[
Zor97
,
EKZ14
]
.
In fact, when the Lyapunov spectrum of the Kontsevich–Zorich cocycle is simple, these deviations can be precisely described.

Kontsevich and Zorich conjectured that the Lyapunov spectrum is simple for all abelian stratum components
[
Zor97
,
Zor99
]
.
Their conjecture extends naturally to the quadratic case as follows.
We form the branched orientation double cover.
The homology of the cover splits into the plus and minus eigenspace for the involution;
the
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
action preserves this splitting.
Simplicity is conjectured in both pieces
[
Zor18
]
.

In the abelian case, Forni proved positivity of the exponents and simplicity in genus two
[
For02
]
.
Simplicity for all abelian strata was later established in the famous work by Avila–Viana
[
AV07a
]
.
Similar techniques were used by Matheus–Möller–Yoccoz
[
MMY15
]
to prove simplicity for certain loci of square-tiled surfaces.
A coding-free proof of this was then given by Eskin–Matheus
[
EM15
]
.

Simplicity in the quadratic case was shown for many stratum components
[
Tre13
,
Gut17
]
.
Our paper establishes simplicity for all abelian and quadratic stratum components; as discussed below, our proof relies on certain machinery of these previous authors, but is independent of their theorems.

Rauzy–Veech groups

The Rauzy–Veech groups are subgroups of the symplectic group generated by the matrices (in a preferred basis) induced by evaluating these cocycles over based loops in Rauzy diagrams.
It follows from
Theorem

5.6
that Rauzy–Veech groups have finite index in the corresponding symplectic monodromy groups. We use this fact to prove the following:

.

For every component of every abelian stratum, the Rauzy–Veech group is Zariski dense in its ambient symplectic group.
Furthermore, for every component of every quadratic stratum, the plus and minus Rauzy–Veech groups are Zariski dense in their corresponding ambient symplectic groups.

The groups of
Theorem

10.1
that arise, by splitting singularities, from abelian strata are known to be finite index inside the ambient symplectic groups (over
\mathbb{Z}
) and hence Zariski dense.
This was shown by Avila–Matheus–Yoccoz
[
AMY18
]
for all abelian hyperelliptic components.
It was extended to all components (abelian or quadratic) that arise from minimal abelian strata by splitting singularites by the fourth author
[
Gut19
,
Gut17
]
.

With
Theorem

5.9
in hand, we can compute the Kontsevich–Zorich cocycle over any loop in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
and not just along the diagonal flow.
It is this additional flexibility that allows us, for the question of Zariski density, to consider a symplectic monodromy group instead of a Rauzy–Veech group.

For the symplectic monodromy groups of abelian differentials, and also for the symplectic monodromy groups induced by the minus piece of the cocycle for quadratic differentials, we directly apply some of Filip’s results to obtain Zariski density
[
Fil17
, Corollary 1.7]
. For the symplectic monodromy groups induced by the plus piece of the cocycle, we need to discuss
algebraic hulls
.

The algebraic hull of the Kontsevich–Zorich cocycle restricted to a linear invariant suborbifold
\mathcal{N}
can be thought of as the smallest algebraic group into which the cocycle over
\mathcal{N}
can be measurably conjugated.
As such, the hull is both an algebro-geometric and an ergodic-theoretic object.
Eskin–Filip–Wright showed that the algebraic hull is as large as it can be, namely it equals the stabiliser of the tautological plane (that is, the cohomology classes spanned by the real and imaginary parts of the differential) in the Zariski closure of the symplectic monodromy group
[
EFW18
, Theorem 1.1]
.
We remark that, just as the theorem by Eskin–Filip–Wright shows that the algebraic hull is as large as it can be,
Theorem

5.9
shows that the flow group is also as large as it can be. Thus, for stratum components,
Theorem

5.9
can be considered as a dynamical analogue of the result by Eskin–Filip–Wright.

The plus piece of the Kontsevich–Zorich cocycle does not meet the tautological plane. The stabiliser then equals the Zariski closure of the symplectic monodromy group, and hence so does the algebraic hull. This result, together with Filip’s classification of the possible Lie algebra representations of algebraic hulls
[
Fil17
, Theorem 1.2]
, enables us to show that the Zariski closure of the symplectic monodromy group of the plus piece is
\Sp(2g,\mathbb{R})
by a simple dimension count.

Simplicity

By the work of Benoist
[
Ben97
]
, Zariski density of an appropriate Rauzy–Veech group implies that the monoids associated with the Kontsevich–Zorich cocycles are “rich” in the sense of the simplicity criterion of Avila–Viana
[
AV07
,
AV07a
]
.
As a consequence of
Theorem

10.1
, we can apply the Avila–Viana criterion to prove the Kontsevich–Zorich simplicity conjecture.

As mentioned before, simplicity was known for all abelian
[
AV07a
]
and some quadratic stratum components
[
Gut17
]
.
It is also known for the principal stratum of quadratic differentials by different methods through the recently announced solution by Eskin–Mirzakhani–Rafi of the Furstenberg problem for random walks on the mapping class group. However, we have claimed the known results as our proof is self-contained and is uniform across all stratum components.

.

The Kontsevich–Zorich cocycle has a simple spectrum for all components of all strata of abelian differentials. The plus and minus Kontsevich–Zorich cocycles also have a simple spectrum for all components of all strata of quadratic differentials.

2.
Strategies

We outline the key steps and ideas in our proofs.

Rooted differentials

The dynamical issues we consider are stable under passing to a finite cover of the given stratum component
\mathcal{C}
.
Accordingly, in
Section

3.6
we pass to the space
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
of
rooted differentials
:
differentials decorated with a choice of horizontal unit tangent vector at a singularity.
We do this for two reasons.
Most importantly, a generic rooted differential admits a canonical decomposition into
zippered rectangles
.
Also, while
\mathcal{C}
has a complicated orbifold structure, the cover
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
is a manifold;
this simplifies various transversality and fundamental group arguments.

Zippered rectangles and the based-loop theorem

The zippered rectangle construction is due to Veech
[
Vee82
]
in the abelian case and due to Boissy–Lanneau
[
BL09
]
in the quadratic case. We discuss them in depth in
Section

4
.
Parameter spaces of zippered rectangles, where the length of base-arc is chosen in a specific way (that we call
distinguished
), define contractible open sets in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
which we call
polytopes of differentials
.
See
Section

4.43
.
The union of these polytopes is dense in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
However, the complement of their union is complicated;
in particular the polytopes do not give
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
a CW-complex structure.
For instance, there are compact arcs in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
that intersect, transversely, the faces of the polytopes infinitely many times.
See
Section

A.1
for an example and relevant discussions.
As a result, our
based-loop theorem
(
Theorem

4.57
) cannot be deduced from naïve transversality arguments.

Fortunately, as discussed by Yoccoz
[
Yoc10
, Proposition in Section 9.3]
, the subset of rooted differentials that do
not
admit any decomposition into zippered rectangles is a countable union of codimension-two subsets. See
4.2
and
Lemma

4.6
.
Thus, any based loop
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
can be homotoped to be disjoint from such differentials.

After this homotopy, we cover the image of
\gamma
by finitely many charts with good properties.
We arrange matters so that the boundaries of these charts are codimension-one embedded submanifolds.
A further homotopy makes
\gamma
transverse to these boundaries while still being covered by the charts.
Unfortunately, our charts may not be contained in any of the polytopes defined above.
That is, the base-arcs along
\gamma
may not be the distinguished base-arcs.
If it is not, we flow; we flow forwards if the base-arc is too short and backwards if the base-arc is too long.

We apply the flow, with direction determined as above, to a sufficiently small subsegment of
\gamma
, contained in a chart.
We then replace the given subsegment of
\gamma
by two segments contained in the flow and one segment contained in the interior of a polytope.
This gives a homotopy of
\gamma
.
Doing this finitely many times, we homotope from
\gamma
to a concatenation of segments which alternate between being diagonal flow segments (forward or backward) or lying inside of polytopes.
This is
Theorem

4.57
, our
based-loop theorem
.

Rauzy–Veech induction and the diagonal flow

A zippered rectangle decomposition gives combinatorics in the form of an
unlabelled irreducible generalised permutation

[
BL09
, Definition 3.1]
.
The decomposition, and thus the rooted differential, can be recovered from the permutation and various associated parameters such as the widths and heights of the rectangles as well as the heights of the zippers.

If we apply the forward diagonal flow, the base-arc grows (exponentially) until it is no longer the distinguished base-arc.
At this point we pass to a new, unique, shorter base-arc which is again a distinguished base-arc.
In this way we obtain a new irreducible generalised permutation as well as new parameters.
We call one such operation a
Rauzy–Veech move
.

The collection of these moves gives a renormalisation procedure known as the
Rauzy–Veech induction
. See
Section

4.60
.

It was originally defined by Rauzy and Veech for abelian differentials
[
Rau79
,
Vee82
]
and by Boissy and Lanneau
[
BL09
]
for quadratic differentials.
Applying the diagonal flow to a single (generic) rooted differential, we obtain a sequence of generalised permutations.
This is the
Rauzy–Veech coding
for the given differential.

The generalised permutations and the Rauzy–Veech moves give an automaton (a directed graph), called the
Rauzy diagram
, as follows.
Two permutations
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are equivalent if we can precompose with a permutation
\sigma
to obtain
\pi\circ\sigma=\pi^{\prime}
.
The vertices of the automaton are equivalence classes of irreducible generalised permutations arising from differentials in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
There is a directed edge from
[\pi]
to
[\rho]
if some representative of the latter arises from a single Rauzy–Veech move applied to some representative of the former.
Furthermore, the Rauzy diagram is strongly connected.
This follows from work of Masur
[
Mas82
]
and Veech
[
Vee82
,
Vee86
]
showing that the diagonal flow is ergodic.

We use the Rauzy–Veech coding to derive simplicity, as explained below.

Flow groups and the fundamental group

Suppose that
\pi
is a vertex in the Rauzy diagram.
Suppose that
q
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
, the polytope of differentials with combinatorics
\pi
.
We define a homomorphism from the fundamental group of the Rauzy diagram (as an undirected graph, based at
\pi
) to
\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}},q)
.
That is, for any directed loop in the Rauzy diagram, based at
\pi
, we choose a diagonal flow segment whose coding is the loop, and cone its endpoints to
q
.
Then strong connectivity of the Rauzy diagram extends this to all based loops.
With this done, we use the based-loop theorem to show that the homomorphism is surjective.
See
Theorem

5.6
.
This answers a question of Yoccoz
[
Yoc10
, Remark in Section 9.3]
.

Suppose that
U
is a contractible open set in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
with a base-point
q
.
From any diagonal flow segment with endpoints in
U
, we obtain an almost-flow loop by coning the endpoints to
q
.
Since shrinking
U
makes the flow group smaller (in principle), we may assume that
U
is contained in a polytope
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
We use the based-loop theorem, and surjectivity, to show that the flow group equals the fundamental group of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
See
Theorem

5.9
.
In other words, at the level of the fundamental group, the diagonal flow captures the topology of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
and hence the topology of
\mathcal{C}
(up to finite index).

Simplicity

By a criterion of Avila–Viana
[
AV07
,
AV07a
]
, simplicity of log-integrable cocycles, such as the Kontsevich–Zorich cocycle, can be deduced from the existence of a coding for the flow that has an “approximate product structure” and a notion of “richness” for the cocycle. A (weaker) version of this coding is stated in
7.19
.
As we indicated earlier, a coding with the required product structure
can be achieved by accelerating the Rauzy–Veech induction.
This was done by Avila–Gouëzel–Yoccoz
[
AGY06
]
for abelian differentials and by Avila–Resende
[
AR12
]
for quadratic differentials.
See
Section

6
for more details.
The remaining task, and the crux of the problem, is to obtain the “richness” of the cocycle.
The required richness was established by Avila–Viana
[
AV07a
]
for abelian stratum components by a direct computation.

By the work of Benoist
[
Ben97
]
, Zariski density in the symplectic group of an associated group implies richness of the cocycle.
In fact, Zariski density is strictly stronger
[
AMY18
, Appendix A]
).

For the Kontsevich–Zorich cocycle, the associated group is the Rauzy–Veech group.
Its Zariski density for hyperelliptic components was proved by Avila–Matheus–Yoccoz.
In fact, their result is stronger; they prove that the Rauzy–Veech group is a certain finite-index subgroup of the ambient symplectic group
[
AMY18
, Theorem 1.1]
.
This finite index result was extended by the fourth author to all components (abelian or quadratic) that arise by splitting zeroes from minima abelian components
[
Gut19
,
Gut17
]
.

Our result on flow groups (
Theorem

5.9
) is a key step in our plan to prove Zariski density of the Rauzy–Veech group, of
any
stratum component.
This is because we can use the cocycle along any loop in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
;
we are not restricted to almost-flow loops.

In recent work
[
Fil17
]
, Filip classifies the situations in which zero Lyapunov exponents can arise, in terms of the Zariski closure of a symplectic monodromy group. See
Section

10.4
. From this description, he also derives the fact that, when restricted to the symplectic block that contains the tautological plane, the Zariski closure of this group is the full symplectic group for this block
[
Fil17
, Corollary 1.7]
.
Combined with this fact, our
Theorem

5.9
directly yields simplicity for abelian components.

A quadratic component lifts to a linear invariant suborbifold of its orientation double-cover.
Hence Filip’s result again applies.
The involution on the orientation double-cover splits the Kontsevich–Zorich cocycle into two symplectically orthogonal blocks, usually referred to as the
plus
(or
invariant
) cocycle and the
minus
(or
anti-invariant
) cocycle.
We will refer to the induced subspaces, inside the relative homology of a fixed flat surface, as the
plus
and
minus piece
, respectively.
The minus piece contains the tautological plane.
Again by Filip’s corollary, the Zariski closure for the minus cocycle is the full symplectic group.
Simplicity of the minus cocycle follows directly from combining this with
Theorem

5.9
. See
Section

10.5
.

It remains to tackle the plus cocycle. Filip also classifies the possible algebraic hulls of any linear invariant suborbifold at the level of Lie algebra represntations
[
Fil17
, Theorem 1.2]
. Moreover, Eskin–Filip–Wright showed that
[
EFW18
, Theorem 1.1]
the algebraic hull and the Zariski closure of a piece of the symplectic monodromy group not containing the tautological place coincide. Hence, this applies for the plus piece.

Now, we exploit our extra flexibility to build a dimension argument that eliminates all but the full symplectic group as the Zariski closure.
We carry out the dimension argument first for components of minimal strata (
Section

11.13
) and hyperelliptic components with two zeros (
Section

11.20
) to conclude Zariski density for the symplectic monodromy groups of these components.
This implies the Zariski density of their Rauzy–Veech groups, as they are finite index in the symplectic monodromy groups (a consequence of
Theorem

5.6
).
We then deal with a few remaining low genera components by using a well-known criterion for Zariski density
[
PR14
]
in
Appendix

B
.
Finally, we extend the density to Rauzy–Veech groups of all quadratic components by standard techniques of surgery/splitting zeroes (
Section

9.10
).
The density allows us to apply the Avila–Viana criterion to conclude the proof of the Kontsevich–Zorich conjecture in full generality.

3.
Preliminaries

3.1.
Differentials and stratum components

Suppose that
P
is a
polygon
:
a compact, convex region in
\mathbb{C}
with boundary a finite union of line segments.
Suppose that
Z=Z(P)
is a finite subset of
P
.
We call
Z
the
marked points
of
P
.
We call the pair
(P,Z)
a
marked polygon
.

A
(completely marked) flat surface

(S,q)
is a finite collection
\{(P,Z(P))\}_{P}
of marked polygons,
together with
side pairings
as follows.

(1)

A side pairing is a (half-)translation which reverse the induced orientation of the paired boundary segments.

(2)

A side pairing between
(P,Z(P))
and
(Q,Z(Q))
must send points of
Z(P)
to points of
Z(Q)
.

(3)

Every boundary segment is part of exactly one side pairing.

The
underlying surface

S
is the quotient of the disjoint union
\bigsqcup_{P}P
by the side pairings.
We deduce that
S
is compact, without boundary, and (canonically) oriented.
We will now abuse notation and use simply
q
to denote our flat surface.
The set of
singularities

Z(q)
is the image, under the quotient map, of
\bigsqcup_{P}Z(P)
.

Topological and geometric properties in the complex plane
\mathbb{C}
, preserved by (half-)translations, descend to flat surfaces.
This applies to orientations, to the real (horizontal) and imaginary (vertical) foliations, to geodesics (line segments), and to angles.
These give orientations,
horizontal
and
vertical
foliations,
flat geodesics
, and
cone angles
to flat surfaces.

A point of
q
with cone angle
k\pi
is a
pole
, a
regular point
, or a
zero
if
k
equals one, two, or more, respectively.
We require that
Z(q)
contain all poles and zeros of
q
.
Note that
Z(q)
may contain (finitely many) regular points – these are the
marked regular points
.
Note that at a regular point the tangent space to
q
is a plane;
at poles and zeros the tangent space is a cone of the corresponding angle.

Definition 3.2
.

Suppose that
q
is a flat surface.
Then
K(q)
is the multi-set of cone angles (divided by
\pi
) appearing at the singularities (that is, points of
Z(q)
).
We call
K(q)
the
cone angle data
for
q
.
∎

Note that the sum
\sum_{k\in K(q)}(k-2)=4g-4
recovers the genus of the underlying surface
S
.
For example, if
Z(q)
contains only marked regular points then
S
is the two-torus.

A (completely marked) flat surface
q
comes equipped with a decomposition into polygons.
Thus a pair of flat surfaces may be isometric, with identical regular marked points and identical vertical and horizontal foliations, yet not be “the same”.
To deal with this, we say that a
differential
is a scissors congruence class of flat surfaces.
We again abuse notation and use simply
q
to denote the differential represented by
(S,q)
.

Definition 3.3
.

The set of differentials sharing the same cone angle data
K
is called a
stratum
.
We equip a stratum with the quotient topology (by scissors congruence) of the subspace topology (enforcing gluings by (half-)translations) of the product topology (coming from the vertices and marked points of the given polygons).
A connected component
\mathcal{C}
of a stratum is called a
(stratum) component
.
∎

In general, a stratum component
\mathcal{C}
is an
orbifold
.
We refer to the book by Boileau–Maillot–Porti
[
BMP03
]
for background on orbifolds and their fundamental groups.
Here are the combinatorial and algebraic invariants shared by all differentials
q
in a fixed stratum component
\mathcal{C}
.

(1)

Abelian

or
quadratic
:
whether or not the vertical foliation of
q
is orientable.

(2)

Singularity data
:
the multi-set

\kappa(q)=\begin{cases}\left\{k-2\mid k\in K(q)\right\},&\mbox{if $q$ is quadratic}\\
\left\{(k-2)/2\mid k\in K(q)\right\},&\mbox{if $q$ is abelian}\end{cases}

Each element of
\kappa(q)
is an integer – in the abelian case this follows from orientability of the vertical foliation.

(3)

Hyperelliptic
: if every
q
lying in
\mathcal{C}
admits an involution with
2g+2
fixed points
[
Lan04
]
.

(4)

Spin
: (only for abelian components where all elments of
\kappa(q)
are even) defined as the Arf invariant of a specific quadratic form
[
Joh80
,
Zor08
]
.

(5)

Regular
or
irregular
: (when possible) distinguished by the dimension of a cohomology group corresponding to a specific divisor
[
CM14
]
.

For the convenience of the reader, we also list the complete classification of abelian and quadratic stratum components in
Section

9.1
.

3.4.
Saddle connections, triangulations, and period coordinates

Suppose that
q
is a differential. A
saddle connection
for
q
is a flat geodesic that meets the singularities
Z(q)
exactly in its endpoints.
(Note that a saddle connection may be a loop.)
By
[
MS91
, Section 4]
, when
Z(q)
is non-empty there is a triangulation of
q
where the vertices are exactly the points of
Z(q)
and where the edges are saddle connections.
If we choose an order on the edges of the triangulation, then their complex lengths give a vector which we call the
(redundant) period coordinates
of
q
.
Note that these coordinates are functions on (small) manifold charts about orbifold points of
\mathcal{C}
.
If
q
is quadratic then there are various ambiguities of sign.
We deal with these in
Section

7.3
.
Also, we give a less redundant version of period coordinates in
Section

4.21
.

3.5.
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-action

The usual action of
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\cong\mathbb{C}
preserves side pairings of polygons and also scissors congruence.
As a result, it descends to an action on differentials, preserving stratum components.
The diagonal part of the
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
–action gives the
diagonal flow
on
\mathcal{C}
.
This is also called the
Teichmüller flow
on
\mathcal{C}
.

By the famous work of Eskin–Mirzakhani–Mohammadi
[
EMM15
]
, closures of
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
–orbits inside of (a certain manifold cover of)
\mathcal{C}
are submanifolds cut out by linear equations (with real coefficients and no constant terms) in period coordinates.
Such an orbit closure is called a
linear invariant submanifold
.

3.6.
Rooted differentials

We now give a manifold cover (of finite degree) of
\mathcal{C}
(essentially following
[
Vee82
, Section 6]
).

Definition 3.7
.

Suppose that
q\in\mathcal{C}
is a differential.
Suppose that
z
lies in
Z(q)
.
Suppose that
v
is a unit tangent vector, at
z
, pointing along some leaf of the horizontal foliation.
We call the pair
(q,v)
a
rooted
differential.
∎

Recalling the difference between the order of a point and the total angle at a point gives a naive count of
4g-4+2|Z(q)|
for the number of rootings of
q
.
However, some rootings of
q
may be equivalent to others when
q
has a symmetry.

Rooted differentials are intended to reproduce the notion of a
marked translation surface
that is widely used in the literature:
see
[
Yoc10
, Section 6.10]
and
[
Boi13
, Section 3]
.
We use
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
to denote the space of rooted differentials.

Lemma 3.8
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is a stratum component with non-empty singularity data.
Then
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
is a manifold.
Furthermore, the map
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}\to\mathcal{C}
forgetting the root is an orbifold covering map of finite degree. ∎

The manifold
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
may not be connected.
This happens, for example, when
q
has singularities of different orders.
We fix any one component;
in a slight abuse of notation, from now on we will call this component
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.

Remark 3.9
.

One drawback of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
is that the
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
–action on
\mathcal{C}
does not lift.
However, the diagonal action does lift, and this is all we will need below.
We finally remark that there is a natural action of the universal cover
{\widetilde{\SL}}(2,\mathbb{R})
on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
∎

4.
Zippered rectangles

Here we pass from the flat geometry of rooted differentials to combinatorics with parameters.
We do this, following Veech
[
Vee82
]
(the abelian case) and Boissy–Lanneau
[
BL09
]
(the quadratic case), using the
zippered rectangle
construction.
Usually, this is used to investigate the dynamics of the diagonal flow on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
However, we are also interested in the topology of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
;
so we present the full details of the zippered rectangle construction and draw particular attention to the aspects we will need.

The
singularity parameters
we use are due to Yoccoz
[
Yoc10
, Section 4.3]
for abelian strata.
These are closely related to the
zipper parameters
introduced by Veech
[
Vee82
, Section 6]
.
In this section we define both parameterisations and discuss how to move between them.

4.1.
Base-arcs

We begin with a version of Keane’s property
[
Kea75
, Section 2]
.
Fix a stratum component
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 4.2
.

Suppose that
q
is a differential in
\mathcal{C}
.
We say that
q
is
saddled
if it has either a horizontal
or
a vertical saddle connection.
We say that
q
is
bi-saddled
if it has both a horizontal
and
a vertical saddle connection.
We denote the sets of saddled and bi-saddled differentials in
\mathcal{C}
by
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{W}
, respectively.
∎

The sets
\mathcal{V}
,
\mathcal{W}
, and their complements lift to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
In a small abuse of notation we will not notationally distinguish the lifts from the originals.

Remark 4.3
.

The set
\mathcal{V}
is a countable union of codimension-one loci,
and
\mathcal{W}
is a countable union of codimension-two loci, in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
∎

Suppose that
(q,v)
is a rooted quadratic differential.
Let
I_{v}
be the horizontal separatrix emanating from the root.
(Note that
I_{v}
may be a horizontal saddle connection, and thus compact.)
We orient
I_{v}
away from the base-point of
v
.
(In our diagrams
I_{v}
is always oriented to the right.)
A segment of a leaf of the vertical foliation, emanating from a point of
I_{v}
, is
upwards
or
downwards
as it makes an angle of
\pi/2
or
-\pi/2
with
I_{v}
.

Suppose that
r
lies in
I_{v}
.
Let
I(r)
be the subarc of
I_{v}
from the base of the root to
r
.
We call
r
the
right endpoint
of
I(r)
.

Definition 4.4
.

We say that
I(r)
is a
base-arc
for
q
if it satisfies the following.

(1)

The interior of
I(r)
meets every vertical saddle connection of
q
.

(2)

If the right endpoint
r
does not lie in
Z(q)
then the upward or downward ray emanating from
r
hits a point of
Z(q)
before hitting
I(r)
a second time.
∎

If
I(r)
is a base-arc for
q
, then
[
Yoc10
, Corollary 5.5]
implies that the interior of
I(r)
meets every leaf of the vertical foliation.

Definition 4.5
.

Suppose that
(q,v)
is a rooted differential.
We define
E(q,v)
to be the set of points
r\in I_{v}
so that
I(r)
is a base-arc.
∎

The proof of the following lemma is analogous to the one given by Yoccoz in the abelian case
[
Yoc10
, Proposition 5.6]
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Suppose that
(q,v)
is a rooted differential.
If
(q,v)
has no vertical saddle connection then
E(q,v)
accumulates at zero.
If
(q,v)
has no horizontal saddle connection then
E(q,v)
is unbounded.

Proof.

Suppose that
q
has no vertical saddle connection.
As in
[
Yoc10
, Corollary 5.5]
the vertical foliation for
q
is minimal (as otherwise the closure of a vertical leaf would be a subsurface with boundary, containing vertical saddle connections
[
Kea75
, Section 2]
).
So every (non-trivial) initial segment of
I_{v}
meets all leaves of the vertical foliation and so satisfies condition (
1
) in
4.4
.
We pass to a subarc to obtain condition (
2
) in
4.4
.
Thus every non-trivial initial segment of
I_{v}
contains a base-arc.

Suppose instead that
q
has no horizontal saddle connection.
So the horizontal foliation for
q
is minimal.
Thus every sufficiently long initial segment of
I_{v}
meets the interior of all vertical saddle connections and so satisfies condition (
1
) in
4.4
.
Extending further gives condition (
2
) in
4.4
.
Thus every initial segment of
I_{v}
is contained in a base-arc.
∎

Definition 4.7
.

Suppose that
I(r)
is a base-arc for
q
.
A point
p
in the interior of
I(r)
is a
top breakpoint
if the upwards ray emanating from
p
hits a singularity before it hits the interior of
I(r)
.
We define the
bottom breakpoints
similarly.
∎

Note that, if some
p
in
I(r)
is both a top and bottom breakpoint, then
q
has a vertical saddle connection.

Definition 4.8
.

The connected components of (the interior of)
I(r)
, minus the top breakpoints, are called the
top intervals
.
The set of these is denoted
I_{\text{t}}=I_{\text{t}}(r)
.
We define the bottom intervals, and the set
I_{\text{b}}=I_{\text{b}}(r)
, similarly.
We define
U\colon\bigsqcup I_{\text{t}}\to I(r)
by setting
U(p)
equal to the first (interior) intersection between the upward ray emanating from
p
and
I(r)
.
We define
D\colon\bigsqcup I_{\text{b}}\to I(r)
similarly.
We finally define
T=(U,D)
:
the
(non-classical) interval exchange transformation
(also called the
linear involution

[
DN88
,
DN90
]
) induced by
I(r)
.
We write
\partial T_{\mathrm{t}}
and
\partial T_{\mathrm{b}}
for the sets of top and bottom breakpoints of
T
, respectively.
∎

Note that, if
q
has enough vertical saddle connections, it can happen that a subinterval of
I(r)
lies in both
I_{\text{t}}
and
I_{\text{b}}
.

Remark 4.9
.

The number of top and bottom breakpoints
|\partial T_{\mathrm{t}}|+|\partial T_{\mathrm{b}}|
equals the number of vertical separatrices emanating from the singularities.
In the abelian case this is proved in
[
Yoc10
, Section 3.1]
.
∎

The next lemma is folklore.

Lemma 4.10
.

Suppose that
(q,v)
be a rooted differential.
Then, considered as a subset of
I_{v}
, the set
E(q,v)
has at most one accumulation point.
Furthermore, if there is an accumulation point then it is the infimum of
E(q,v)
(and, in this case, the infimum is not an element of
E(q,v)
).

Proof.

Suppose, for a contradiction, that there is an increasing sequence
(r_{n})_{n}
in
E(q,v)
converging to some point
x
in
I_{v}
.
Thus, none of the
r_{n}
are singularities (that is, lie in
Z(q)
).
By definition, either the upward or downward ray emanating from
r_{n}
hits a singularity before it hits the interior of
I(r_{n})
.
Breaking symmetry (and passing to a subsequence and reindexing as needed) we may assume that the
upward
ray from
r_{n}
hits a singularity (and so does not meet
I(r_{n})
on its interior).

Suppose that there is a subsequence of the indices
(n_{k})_{k}
so that the points
s_{k}=r_{n_{k}}
have the following property:

•

the upward rays from
s_{k}
hit a singularity before hitting the interior of
I(x)-I(s_{k})
.

Then it follows that there is no uniform upper bound on the number of top breakpoints for the interval exchange transformations induced on the base-arcs
I(s_{k})
.
This contradicts
4.9
.
After reindexing to eliminate finitely many of the
r_{n}
we deduce:

•

for all
y
in
[r_{0},x)
the upward ray from
y
hits
I(x)-I(y)
before hitting a singularity.

Thus the upward flow takes the interval
[r_{0},x)
isometrically, and strictly, inside of itself.
This is a contradiction.

Suppose instead that there is an decreasing sequence
(r_{n})_{n}
in
I_{v}
converging to
x
.
Breaking symmetry, we may assume that there is a subsequence of indices
(n_{k})_{k}
so that the points
s_{k}=r_{n_{k}}
have the following property:

s_{k+1}<U_{k}(s_{k+1})<s_{k}

Here
T_{k}=(U_{k},D_{k})
is the interval exchange transformation induced on
I(s_{k})
.
We deduce that
U_{k}(s_{k+1})-s_{k+1}
converges to zero.
Let
\alpha_{k}
be the upwards segment from
s_{k+1}
to
U_{k}(s_{k+1})
.
From the discreteness of the saddle spectrum we deduce that the length of
\alpha_{k}
tends to infinity with
k
.

If
x
(the limit of the
s_{k}
) is the base-point of
v
then there is nothing to prove.
Suppose instead that the interval
I(x)
is non-trivial.
The upwards segment
\alpha_{k}
does not intersect
I(x)
.
Thus the Hausdorff limit in
q
of
(\alpha_{k})_{k}
is a closed subsurface, which we denote by
X\subseteq S
.
We note that
X
is disjoint from the interior of
I(x)
.
Thus
X
is not all of
S
.
So
\partial X
is non-empty;
we deduce that
\partial X
is a union of vertical saddle connections.
Since none of these intersect the interior of
I(x)
we deduce that
I(x)
is not a base-arc.
∎

4.11.
Labelled generalised permutations

Suppose that
(q,v)
is a rooted differential.
Recall that
I_{v}
is oriented by
v
, giving upwards and downwards rays in the vertical foliation.
Suppose that
r
lies in
E(q,v)
.
Thus
I(r)\subseteq I_{v}
is a base-arc.
Let
T=(U,D)
be the (non-classical) interval exchange transformation induced by
I(r)
.
Note that
T
gives a fixed-point free involution on
I_{\text{t}}\sqcup I_{\text{b}}
as follows:

T(J)=\begin{cases}U(J),&\mbox{if $J$ is a top interval}\\
D(J),&\mbox{if $J$ is a bottom interval}\end{cases}

We deduce that
|I_{\text{t}}|+|I_{\text{b}}|
is even.
Set
2d=|I_{\text{t}}|+|I_{\text{b}}|
.

We now code the action of
T
on the top and bottom intervals.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a set of
d

letters
.
Let
\ell=|I_{\text{t}}|
and
m=|I_{\text{b}}|
;
so
2d=\ell+m
.
We index the intervals of
I_{\text{t}}
(and of
I_{\text{b}}
) according to their order along the base-arc
I=I(r)
.
So
I_{\text{t}}=(J_{i})_{i=1}^{\ell}
and
I_{\text{b}}=(J_{i})_{i=\ell+1}^{\ell+m}
.
Now let
\pi=\pi_{T}\colon\{1,2,\ldots,2d\}\to\mathcal{A}
be any two-to-one map with the following property:
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
, if
\{i,j\}=\pi^{-1}(a)
then
T(J_{i})=J_{j}
.
Thus
\pi
induces a fixed-point free involution
\sigma
of
\{1,2,\ldots,2d\}
;
here
\sigma(i)=j
if and only if
\pi(i)=\pi(j)
(and
i\neq j
).

We call
(\pi,\ell,m)
a
labelled generalised permutation
of
\mathcal{A}
.
We say that
(\pi,\ell,m)

models
the (non-classical) interval exchange transformation
T
.
We often just write
\pi
, suppressing
\ell
and
m
from the notation.
Also, we often omit
T
and simply say that
\pi

models
the data
(q,v,I(r))
.

Generalised permutations were first considered by Danthony–Nogueira
[
DN88
,
DN90
]
and then by Boissy–Lannaeu
[
BL09
, Definition 2.4]
.
We adopt the notation and language of the latter.

Definition 4.12
.

Suppose that
(\pi,\ell,m)
and
(\pi^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},m^{\prime})
are generalised permutations on
\mathcal{A}
.
We say they are
equivalent
if
m=m^{\prime}
, if
\ell=\ell^{\prime}
, and if there is a reindexing
s\in\Sym(\mathcal{A})
so that
\pi^{\prime}=s\circ\pi
.
We call the equivalence class
[\pi]
an
unlabelled
generalised permutation.
∎

The equivalence classes of
4.12
are sometimes referred to as
reduced permutations
(for example by Boissy
[
Boi13
]
and
[
Boi20
]
).

For labelled generalised permutations, Boissy–Lanneau
[
BL09
, Definition 3.1]
introduce the notion of
combinatorially irreducible
.
We do not reproduce the definition here.
Instead we note one of their main results
[
BL09
, Theorem 3.2]
which is crucial in our work.

Theorem 4.13
.

A labelled generalised permutation is combinatorially irreducible if and only if it models a rooted differential with a choice of base-arc. ∎

Let
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{root}}
be the set of unlabelled generalised permutations arising from rooted differentials (equipped with base-arcs) in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
We call
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{root}}
the
Rauzy class
of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
(This is sometimes called the
reduced
Rauzy class.)
We call the set
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{lab}}

of labelled permutations the
labelled Rauzy class
.
By
Theorem

4.13
, as we vary over all stratum components and all choices of rootings, all labelled irreducible generalised permutations arise from the above construction
[
BL09
, Theorem 3.2]
.

Suppose that
(\pi,\ell,m)
is a labelled generalised permutation of
\mathcal{A}
.
The letters
\pi(1),\ldots,\pi(\ell)
are the
top letters
for
\pi
.
Similarly, the letters
\pi(\ell+1),\ldots,\pi(\ell+m)
are the
bottom letters
.
Any letter that is both a top letter and a bottom letter is called a
translation letter
.
Any letter that is only a top letter (or only a bottom letter) is called a
flip letter
.
We explain the terminology below.

We say that
\pi
is a
abelian permutation
if it has no flip letters.
We say that
\pi
is a
quadratic permutation
it has (at least one) top flip letter and (at least one) bottom flip letter.
All generalised permutations that arising in
Theorem

4.13
are of one of these two types.

From now on, we will not use the terminology “generalised permutation”.
Instead we collectively refer to abelian and quadratic permutations simply as
permutations
.

4.14.
The rectangles

Suppose that
(q,v)
is a rooted differential.
Suppose that
I(r)
is a base-arc for
q
.
Suppose that the labelled permutation
\pi
models the data
(q,v,I(r))
.

Suppose, as above, that
I_{\text{t}}=(J_{i})_{i=1}^{\ell}
are the top intervals and
I_{\text{b}}=(J_{i})_{i=\ell+1}^{\ell+m}
are the bottom intervals.
Fix
i\leqslant 2d
;
let
j=\sigma(i)
and let
\alpha=\pi(i)=\pi(j)
.
If
i\leqslant\ell
then
T(J_{i})=U(J_{i})=J_{j}
: that is, the upwards flow restricted to
J_{i}
has first return (to
I
) equal to
J_{j}
.
We deduce that the return time is constant on
J_{i}
.
On the other hand, if
i>\ell
then the points of
J_{i}
flow downwards to land simultaneously in
J_{j}
.
In a slight abuse of notation we call the closure of the resulting vertical strip a
rectangle
;
we denote it by
R_{\alpha}
.
As an additional abuse of notation we use
\partial R_{\alpha}
to denote
J_{i}\cup J_{j}
together with the correctly chosen vertical segments connecting the endpoints of
J_{i}
to those of
J_{j}
.
While
R_{\alpha}
may not be an embedded closed rectangle, the set
R_{\alpha}-\partial R_{\alpha}
is an embedded open rectangle.

Definition 4.15
.

We lay out the base-arc
I(r)
in the plane, placing the root at the origin and
I(r)
itself on the positive real axis.
Suppose that
\pi(i)=\pi(j)=\alpha
with
i\neq j
.
There are two resulting layouts of
R_{\alpha}
which we denote by
R_{i}
and
R_{j}
.
The sides of these layouts receive
cardinal directions
: south, east, north and west.
If
\alpha
is a translation letter then the translation taking
R_{i}
to
R_{j}
preserves the cardinal directions.
If
\alpha
is a flip letter then the resulting half-translation exchanges south and north as well as east and west.
∎

Corollary 4.16
.

The closure of the west side of any rectangle
R_{i}
contains at most one singularity.
The same holds for the east side of any
R_{i}
. ∎

The vertical sides of rectangles are glued across certain sub-arcs called
zippers
, which we now define.

Figure 4.17
.
A zippered rectangle construction belonging to the permutation
\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2&3&1&2&4&5&6\\
&3&5&4&7&6&7\end{smallmatrix}\big)
.
The singularities are a regular point (blue dot) and a zero of cone angle
10\pi
(yellow dot).
Note that every zipper appears as a solid (green) arc exactly once.
Also, all rectangles appear twice
except

R_{6}
which appears three times.
This is because the zipper through the right endpoint of
I
is taller than
R_{7}
.

4.18.
The zippers

Suppose that
(q,v)
is a rooted differential.
Suppose that
I(r)
is a base-arc for
q
.
Let
T=(U,D)
be the (non-classical) interval exchange transformation induced on
I(r)
.
Let
\pi
be a labelled permutation that models
T
.

Suppose that
p
is an top breakpoint for
T
;
that is, the upward ray emanating from
p
hits a singularity before it returns to the interior of
I
.
We denote this upward segment by
\mathrm{zip}_{\mathrm{t}}(p)
and we call it a
top zipper
.
By construction
\mathrm{zip}_{\mathrm{t}}(p)
meets the interior of
I
only in the point
p
.
Also by construction the boundary of
\mathrm{zip}_{\mathrm{t}}(p)
is
p
itself and some singularity
z_{\mathrm{t}}(p)
in
Z(q)
.
We make similar definitions when
p
lies in
\partial T_{\mathrm{b}}
to obtain
\mathrm{zip}_{\mathrm{b}}(p)
and
z_{\mathrm{b}}(p)
.

Lemma 4.19
.

Suppose that
p
is a (top or bottom) breakpoint.
Suppose that
\mathrm{zip}(p)
is the zipper for
p
.
Let
z(p)
be the other endpoint of
\mathrm{zip}(p)
.

•

Suppose that the right endpoint of
I(r)
does not lie in the interior of
\mathrm{zip}(p)
.
Then the interior of
\mathrm{zip}(p)
meets two sides of distinct rectangles
R_{i}
and
R_{j}
(of the layout) west and east of
\mathrm{zip}(p)
, respectively.
Note that
\pi(i)=\pi(j)
if and only if
z(p)
is a pole.

•

Suppose that the right endpoint
r
lies in the interior of
\mathrm{zip}(p)
.
Then the interior of
\mathrm{zip}(p)
meets (in
q
) three sides of three rectangles.
Two of these rectangles are in the layout and are to the west of
\mathrm{zip}(p)
;
the third is not in the layout.
In this case
r
is not a singularity and
\mathrm{zip}(p)
is the unique zipper that contains
r
.

Proof.

Suppose that the right endpoint
r
does not lie in the interior of
\mathrm{zip}(p)
.
Then the rectangle heights (to the left and right of
\mathrm{zip}(p)
) are greater than or equal to the zipper height.
If the rectangle on the left has the same label as the rectangle on the right then
\mathrm{zip}(p)
lies on the unique vertical separatrix through
z(p)
;
hence
z(p)
is a pole.

Suppose that the right endpoint
r
lies in the interior of
\mathrm{zip}(p)
.
It follows from the definition of zippers that
r
is not a singularity.
Let
R
and
R^{\prime}
be the rectangles meeting
p
.
Let
R
be the rectangle with height equal to the distance (along the zipper) between
p
and
r
.
In this case
R^{\prime}
has height greater than or equal to the zipper height, and there is a rectangle
R^{\prime\prime}
, stacked on top of
R
(see
R_{4}
,
R_{6}
and
R_{7}
below the base-arc in
Figure

4.17
), so that the height of
R
plus the height of
R^{\prime\prime}
is greater than or equal to the zipper height.
Conversely, if the interior of a zipper meets three rectangles, consider the side where it meets two rectangles.
Then the horizontal boundary shared by the two rectangles is a sub-arc of the base-arc, hence it must meet the zipper in the point
r
.
∎

Corollary 4.20
.

Suppose that
\pi(i)=\pi(j)=\alpha
with
i\neq j
.
Then at least one of the rectangles
R_{i}
and
R_{j}
(or perhaps both) has a singularity in the closure of its west side.
∎

4.21.
Singularity parameters

Suppose that
(q,v)
is a rooted differential;
suppose that
I(r)
is a base-arc for
(q,v)
.
Suppose that
\pi
models the data
(q,v,I(r))
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
denote the finite alphabet for
\pi
.

We now define the
singularity parameters

(x_{q},y_{q})\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{A}}
from the given zippered rectangle decomposition of
q
.
At the same time we define certain arcs
\gamma_{i}
, typically contained in the interior of their rectangle
R_{i}
.

Fix a letter
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
.
Suppose that
\pi(i)=\pi(j)=\alpha
with
i\neq j
.
Applying
Corollary

4.20
, and swapping
i
and
j
if needed, we may suppose that
R_{i}
has a singularity
z_{\mathrm{w}}
in the closure of its west side.
Let
K_{i}
be the eastward horizontal spanning arc of
R_{i}
, emanating from
z_{\mathrm{w}}
.
So
K_{i}
ends in the east side of
R_{i}
.

Definition 4.22
.

We define the
width
of
\alpha
to be

x_{\alpha}=|K_{i}|\qed

We deduce that
x_{\alpha}
is the width of
R_{i}
and thus the width of
R_{\alpha}
.
For future use, we note that the widths of rectangles are positive.
That is,

(4.23)

x_{\alpha}>0

Let
p_{i}
be the endpoint of
K_{i}
lying in the east side of
R_{i}
.

•

If there is a singularity in the closure of the east side of
R_{i}
(by
Corollary

4.16
there is at most one) then we denote it by
z_{\mathrm{e}}
.
See
Figures

27(a)
and
27(b)
.

•

Suppose instead that there is no singularity in the closure of the east side of
R_{i}
.
In this case we extend the east side of
R_{i}
away from
J_{i}\subseteq I(r)
until it meets a singularity, which we denote
z_{\mathrm{e}}
.
(Note that this happens before the extension meets the interior of
I(r)
, since
I(r)
is a base-arc.)
See
Figure

27(c)
.

In either case we define
L_{i}
to be the oriented vertical segment between
p_{i}
and
z_{\mathrm{e}}
, oriented away from
p_{i}
.
We define
\sgn(L_{i})
to be plus one if
L_{i}
points north and minus one if
L_{i}
points south.

Definition 4.24
.

We define the
height
of
\alpha
to be

y_{\alpha}=\sgn(L_{i})\cdot|L_{i}|

So
y_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}
is the signed height of
z_{\mathrm{e}}
relative to
z_{\mathrm{w}}
.
∎

With
R_{i}
and
R_{j}
as above, we now consider the possibility that
R_{j}
is the (unique) rectangle which does
not
have a singularity in the closure of its west side.
In this case
\alpha
is a flip letter and
R_{i}
was a rightmost rectangle in our layout.
Let
f_{\alpha}\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
be the half-translation taking
R_{i}
to
R_{j}
.
We define
K_{j}
and
L_{j}
to be
f_{\alpha}(K_{i})
and
f_{\alpha}(L_{i})
, respectively, reversing orientation in both cases.

Definition 4.25
.

The pair
(x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha})
is the
singularity parameter
of the letter
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
.
∎

Note that the singularity parameter is well-defined because the two rectangles
R_{i}
and
R_{j}
(and their various singularities) differ by a (half-)translation.

Definition 4.26
.

We define the oriented arc
\gamma_{i}=K_{i}\cup L_{i}
to be the
rectilinear arc
associated with the rectangle
R_{i}
.
∎

(a)

The curve
\gamma_{i}
turns left at
p_{i}
and can be tightened to a saddle connection.

(b)

The curve
\gamma_{i}
turns right at
p_{i}
and can be tightened to a saddle connection.

(c)

The curve
\gamma_{i}
may or may not tighten to a saddle connection.

Figure 4.27
.
Three cases of singularity parameters.

Remark 4.28
.

Suppose that
\gamma_{i}
is contained in the closure of
R_{i}
.
Then
\gamma_{j}
is contained in the closure of
R_{j}
.
In this case
\gamma_{i}
is, up to a translation,
identical to
\gamma_{j}
(contained in
R_{j}
).
It follows that, again in this case,
\gamma_{i}
can be straightened to give a saddle connection in
R_{i}
.
See
Figures

27(a)
and
27(b)
.
The parameter
x_{\alpha}+\mathrm{i}y_{\alpha}
is then the period of
\gamma_{i}
.

In general there is at most one letter (in both the abelian and quadratic setting) where
\gamma_{i}
(and thus
\gamma_{j}
) may not be contained in the closure of
R_{i}
.
In this case
\gamma_{i}
may or may not be homotopic (relative to its endpoints) to a saddle connection.
See
Figure

27(c)
for the general situation and
Figure

4.17
for a particular example.
This explains the delicacy of the definitions of the rectilinear arcs
\gamma_{i}
and of the parameters
y_{\alpha}
.
∎

Remark 4.29
.

We further deduce that
\gamma_{i}
is homotopic, relative to
Z(q)
, to
\gamma_{j}
when
\alpha
is a translation letter and is homotopic to the orientation-reverse of
\gamma_{j}
when
\alpha
is a flip letter.
∎

Definition 4.30
.

Set
x_{q}=(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
and
y_{q}=(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
.
We call
(x_{q},y_{q})
the
singularity parameters
induced by the data
(q,v,I(r))
.
∎

4.31.
Width and height equalities

We now form two larger rectilinear arcs in the plane:

\gamma_{\mathrm{t}}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}\qquad\qquad\gamma_{\mathrm{b}}=\bigcup_{i=\ell+1}^{\ell+m}\gamma_{i}

Both start at the root and end at the eastmost singularity (on the same vertical leaf as
r
).
Since they have the same beginning and end, we deduce that their periods in
q
are equal.
We call this the
period equality
.
Taking real and imaginary parts, we deduce the following:

\sum_{i\leqslant\ell}x_{\pi(i)}=\sum_{i>\ell}x_{\pi(i)},\qquad\qquad\sum_{i\leqslant\ell}y_{\pi(i)}=\sum_{i>\ell}y_{\pi(i)}

Since translation letters contribute to both sides, the period equality gives the
width
and
height equalities
:

(4.32)

\sum_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}=\sum_{\beta}x_{\beta},\qquad\qquad\sum_{\alpha}y_{\alpha}=\sum_{\beta}y_{\beta}

where
\alpha
ranges over the top flip letters and
\beta
ranges over the bottom flip letters.

4.33.
Masur polygon

Suppose that a labelled permutation
\pi
models the data
(q,v,I(r))
.
We use the notion of rectilinear arcs as given in
4.26
.
Suppose that we are in the special situation where every rectilinear arc
\gamma_{i}
, when transported to
q
, tightens to give a saddle connection.
In this case we may do the following:

•

straighten all of the
\gamma_{i}
(in
\mathbb{C}
) to obtain line segments
\gamma^{*}_{i}
,

•

cut
\mathbb{C}
along the
\gamma^{*}_{i}
, and

•

obtain a polygon
M=M(q,v,I(r))
.

We call
M
the
Masur polygon
for
q
, induced by
I(r)
.
When
q
is abelian, the periods of the saddle connections
\gamma^{*}_{i}
give the
period coordinates
for
q
.
When
q
is quadratic, the choice of root
v
determines the signs of the periods of the
\gamma^{*}_{i}
.
In the quadratic case these periods satisfy the single complex relation given by
Equation

4.32
.

When some
\gamma_{i}
cannot be straightened, the Masur polygon
M
, as induced by
I(r)
, does not exist.
For a discussion and further examples see
Appendix

C
.

4.34.
Zipper parameters

Again with notation as above, we break symmetry and pick
p\in\partial T_{\mathrm{t}}
.
We assume that
p\neq r\times\{\mathrm{t}\}
if
r\in\partial T_{\mathrm{t}}
.
Let
\mathrm{zip}(p)
be a top zipper based at
p
.
By a slight abuse of notation, think of
p
as a point in
I
.
Let
R_{\pi(i)}
for
i\leqslant\ell
be the rectangle to the left of
\mathrm{zip}(p)
.
Then the horizontal coordinate of
p
(that is, the distance of
p
from the left-endpoint of
I
) is given by

x(p)=\sum_{j=1}^{i}x_{\pi(j)}.

The height of
\mathrm{zip}(p)
is given by

(4.35)

h(\mathrm{zip}(p))=\sum_{j=1}^{i}y_{\pi(j)}.

and we require this to be positive.
This gives us the
top zipper inequalities

(4.36)

\sum_{j=1}^{i}y_{\pi(j)}>0

for all
i<\ell
.

Similarly, if
\mathrm{zip}(p)
for
p\in\partial T_{\mathrm{b}}\times\{\mathrm{b}\}
and
p\neq r\times\{\mathrm{b}\}
if
r\in\partial T_{\mathrm{b}}
is a bottom zipper and
R_{\pi(i)}
for
i\geqslant\ell+1
then the horizontal coordinate is

x(p)=\sum_{j=\ell+1}^{i}x_{\pi(j)}

and the height is

(4.37)

h(\mathrm{zip}(p))=\sum_{j=\ell+1}^{i}y_{\pi(j)}.

Since it is a bottom zipper, we require that
h(\mathrm{zip}(p))
is negative.
This gives us the
bottom zipper inequalities

(4.38)

\sum_{j=\ell+1}^{i}y_{\pi(j)}<0

for all
\ell+1\leqslant i<\ell+m
.

It remains to consider the right endpoint
r
. The zipper height of
\mathrm{zip}(r)
gives us a linear relation in the
y
parameters.
Note that the above equalities express the height
h(\mathrm{zip}(r))
in two ways; namely

h(\mathrm{zip}(r))=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}y_{\pi(j)}

and

h(\mathrm{zip}(r))=\sum_{j=\ell+1}^{\ell+m}y_{\pi(j)}.

We thus recover the
height
equality

(4.39)

\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}y_{\pi(k)}=\sum_{k=\ell+1}^{\ell+m}y_{\pi(k)}

which is equivalent to
\sum y_{\alpha}=\sum y_{\beta}
,
where
\alpha
ranges over the top flip letters and
\beta
ranges over the bottom flip letters.

The height and width equalities are identical in form.
Thus, the dimensions of the space of
x
and
y
parameters are equal;
they are
|\mathcal{A}|
in the abelian case and
|\mathcal{A}|-1
in the quadratic case.

4.40.
Rectangle parameters

For all rectangles
R=R_{\alpha}
, at least one of the points
z_{\mathrm{e}}
and
z_{\mathrm{w}}
lie in its east and west sides, respectively.
Breaking symmetry, suppose that
\alpha
is a top letter and
z_{\mathrm{e}}
lies in its east side.
Let
\mathrm{zip}(p)
for
p\in\partial T_{\mathrm{t}}\times\{\mathrm{t}\}
be the zipper with end point
z_{\mathrm{e}}
.
If
\alpha
is a translation letter then there is a zipper
\mathrm{zip}(p^{\prime})
for
p^{\prime}\in\partial T_{\mathrm{b}}\times\{\mathrm{b}\}
with endpoint
z_{\mathrm{e}}
such that the union
\mathrm{zip}(p)\cup\mathrm{zip}(p^{\prime})
is the east side of
R_{\alpha}
.
Recall that the heights of bottom zippers are negative.
Hence, the height
h(R_{\alpha})
satisfies

(4.41)

h(R_{\alpha})=h(\mathrm{zip}(p))-h(\mathrm{zip}(p^{\prime})).

If
\alpha
is a flip letter instead then there is a zipper
\mathrm{zip}(p^{\prime})
for
p^{\prime}\in\partial T_{\mathrm{t}}\times\{\mathrm{t}\}
with end point
z_{\mathrm{e}}
such that the union
\mathrm{zip}(p)\cup\mathrm{zip}(p^{\prime})
is the east side of
R_{\alpha}
.
The height
h(R_{\alpha})
is then

(4.42)

h(R_{\alpha})=h(\mathrm{zip}(p))+h(\mathrm{zip}(p^{\prime})).

A similar discussion follows if
\alpha
is a bottom letter.

4.43.
Polytopes of differentials and polytopes of parameters

Definition 4.44
.

Suppose that
(q,v)
is a rooted differential.
Suppose that

•

1\notin E(q,v)
and

•

there exist
s,t\in E(q,v)
with
s<1<t
.

In this case
Lemma

4.10
tells us that
r=\min(E(q,v)\cap(1,\infty))
is well-defined.
We call
I(r)
the
distinguished base-arc
for
(q,v)
.
∎

The choice of
1
in the above definition is simply a choice, and can be replaced by any other positive real number.

Definition 4.45
.

Suppose that
\pi
is a labelled permutation.
We define
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
, the
polytope of differentials
modelled by
\pi
, as follows.
Suppose that
(q,v)
is a rooted differential.
Suppose that
(q,v)
has a distinguished base-arc
I(r)
.
Suppose that
(q,v,I(r))
is modelled by
\pi
.
Then we place
(q,v)
into the set
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
∎

We deduce the following.

Lemma 4.46
.

Suppose that
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are labelled permutations.
If
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are equivalent then
\mathcal{C}(\pi)=\mathcal{C}(\pi^{\prime})
.
If
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are not equivalent then
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
and
\mathcal{C}(\pi^{\prime})
are disjoint. ∎

Lemma 4.47
.

Suppose that
\pi
is a labelled permutation.
Then the polytope
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
is open.

Proof.

Fix
(q,v)
in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
Suppose that
I(r)
is the given distinguished base-arc for
(q,v)
.
We use
I(r)
to give a layout of
q
in the plane, made of the rectangles found in
Section

4.14
.
Let
I(0,1)
be the open initial segment of
I(r)
of length one.
Let
z_{\mathrm{e}}
be the easternmost singularity in this layout.
Let
\mathrm{zip}(p)
and
\mathrm{zip}(p^{\prime})
be the zippers contained in the northern and southern separatrices emanating from
z_{\mathrm{e}}
(as in the layout).
By
Lemma

4.19
there are rectangles
R
and
R^{\prime}

not
in the layout whose sides contain
\mathrm{zip}(p)
and
\mathrm{zip}(p^{\prime})
respectively.

Note that the breakpoints (as in
4.7
) lie in
I(0,1)
.
The top breakpoints are all distinct as are the bottom breakpoints.
Note also that the zippers have positive heights.
We deduce, in a sufficiently small neighbourhood of
(q,v)
, that

•

no pair top breakpoints collide,

•

no pair of bottom breakpoints collide, and

•

no singularity (except its westernmost endpoint) meets
I(0,1)
.

In particular, the widths of the rectangles (including
R
and
R^{\prime}
) are bounded away from zero.
We deduce that all differentials near
(q,v)
also have distinguished base-arcs and that these also induce the labelled permutation
\pi
.
∎

Lemma 4.48
.

The union of the polytopes
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
is dense in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.

Proof.

By
4.3
the set
\mathcal{V}
(of saddled differentials) is a countable union of codimension-one subsets.
Let
\mathcal{O}
be the subset of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
of differentials
(q,v)
where
1
lies in
E(q,v)
.
So
\mathcal{O}
is also a countable union of codimension-one subsets.
Thus
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}-(\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{O})
is dense.

Suppose that
(q,v)
is a rooted differential in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}-(\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{O})
.
By
Lemma

4.6
, the
E(q,v)
accumulates at zero and is also unbounded.
Thus
E(q,v)
contains values both smaller and larger than one, but does not contain one.
Thus
(q,v)
has a distinguished base-arc and so lies in some
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
, as desired.
∎

Remark 4.49
.

In
Appendix

D
, we recall the construction of the Masur–Smillie–Veech measure on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
. The subset
\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{O}
has measure zero, and hence the union of polytopes has full measure.
∎

We now discuss the
polytope of parameters
.

Definition 4.50
.

Suppose that
\pi
is a labelled permutation.
The
polytope of parameters

P(\pi)
is the set of pairs
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{A}}
satisfying the following:

(1)

the positivity inequalities (
4.23
),

(2)

the width and height equalities (
4.32
),

(3)

the zipper inequalities (
4.36
) and (
4.38
), and

(4)

the
distinguished base-arc inequalities

(4.51)

1<\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}x_{\pi(i)}<1+\min\{x_{\pi(\ell)},x_{\pi(\ell+m)}\}

We say that parameters
(x,y)\in P(\pi)
are
admissible
for
\pi
.
∎

Lemma 4.52
.

Suppose that
\pi
is a labelled permutation.
Suppose that
(q,v)
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
Suppose that
I(r)
is the distinguished base-arc for
(q,v)
.
Let
(x_{q},y_{q})
be the singularity parameters induced by the data
(q,v,I(r))
.
Then
(x_{q},y_{q})
lies in
P(\pi)
.

Proof.

Since
\pi
models the data
(q,v,I(r)
, the parameters
(x,y)
satisfy
the positivity inequalities (
4.23
) (shown in
Section

4.21
),
the width and height equalities (
4.32
) (shown in
Section

4.31
), and
the zipper inequalities (
4.36
) and (
4.38
) (shown in
Section

4.34
).

Define
r^{\prime}
to be the maximum of
E(q,v)\cap(0,1)
.
Note that
r^{\prime}
is well-defined because
(q,v)
has a distinguished base-arc and by
Lemma

4.10
.
Recall that
r
is the minimum of
E(q,v)\cap(1,\infty)
.
Thus
r^{\prime}<1<r
.
Suppose that
\pi
is of the form
(\pi,\ell,m)
;
that is, there are
\ell
letters on the top and
m
letters on the bottom.
Since
r^{\prime}
and
r
are consecutive points in
E(q,v)
, we have that

r=r^{\prime}+\min\{x_{\pi(\ell)},x_{\pi(\ell+m)}\}<1+\min\{x_{\pi(\ell)},x_{\pi(\ell+m)}\}

as required.
∎

Definition 4.53
.

Suppose that
\pi
is a labelled permutation.
Suppose that
(q,v)
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
Let
I(r)
be the resulting distinguished base-arc.
We define
p_{\pi}\colon\mathcal{C}(\pi)\to P(\pi)
by setting
p_{\pi}(q,v)=(x_{q},y_{q})
.
∎

We now give a function in the opposite direction.

Definition 4.54
.

Suppose that
\pi
is a labelled permutation.
Suppose that
(x,y)
lies in
P(\pi)
(so is admissible for
\pi
).
We now layout the resulting rectangles along the non-negative real axis in
\mathbb{C}
(as in
Section

4.14
).
We glue along the zippers to obtain the differential
q_{\pi}(x,y)
.
∎

It is a delicate result of Boissy–Lanneau
[
BL09
, Lemma 2.12]
that the function
q_{\pi}
is well-defined.
Since the parameters are admissible,
q_{\pi}(x,y)
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.

From the definitions, the functions
q_{\pi}
and
p_{\pi}
are inverses of each other.
Thus both are bijections.

Lemma 4.55
.

Suppose that
\pi
is a labelled permutation.
Then the maps
q_{\pi}
and
p_{\pi}
are continuous.
Thus both are homeomorphisms.

Proof.

Fix parameters
(x,y)
in
P(\pi)
.
A small change
(x,y)
, remaining in
P(\pi)
, gives a small motion of the associated rectangles and zippers;
this leads to a small motion of the polygons, and thus by
3.3
remains in a small open set in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
Thus
q_{\pi}
is continuous.

Fix instead a differential
(q,v)
in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
Since
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
is open (
Lemma

4.47
) a sufficiently small deformation of
(q,v)
remains in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
Thus we may ignore the quotient (by scissors congruence) appearing in
3.3
.
Thus a sufficiently small deformation of
(q,v)
only moves the vertices (of the polygons) a small amount.
Since we remain in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
the shapes of the rectangles and zippers change continuously;
thus the parameters change continuously.
So
p_{\pi}
is continuous, as desired.
∎

4.56.
Based loops in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}

We now state our key tool for relating “topology” to “dynamics”.
Roughly, every (based) loop in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
can (almost) be homotoped to be a concatenation of diagonal flow segments.
In the statement and proof we suppress the notation
v
for the root.

Theorem 4.57
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
is a stratum component of rooted quadratic differentials.
Suppose that
q_{0}
is a base-point in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
Suppose that
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
is a loop based at
q_{0}
.
Then, up to a homotopy relative to the base-point, the loop
\gamma
can be written as a finite concatenation of paths that are either

•

geodesic segments for the diagonal flow or

•

contained inside some polytope of differentials.

Proof.

Recall that the set
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
of bi-saddled rooted differentials is a countable union of codimension-two loci.
For convenience, we assume that our basepoint
q_{0}
is contained in some polytope but does not lie in
\mathcal{W}
.

Let
q
be another differential in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}-\mathcal{W}
.
By
Lemma

4.6
, we may choose a base-arc
I
for
q
.
With
I
chosen, we obtain a (generalised) permutation
\pi
and singularity parameters
(x,y)
.
Appealing to
[
BL09
, Theorem 3.2]
, the permutation
\pi
is irreducible (in the sense of
[
BL09
, Definition 3.1]
).

By
Lemma

4.10
, the induced singularity parameters give coordinates in a neighbourhood of
q
.
So there exists an open set in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
containing
q
given by the zippered rectangles construction with underlying permutation
\pi
.
If
q
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
then there is an (even smaller) neighbourhood
U(q)
of
q
with the following properties:

(1)

U(q)
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
and

(2)

q^{-1}_{\pi}(U(q))
is a product neighbourhood in
P(\pi)
.

We call
U(q)
an
admissible box neighbourhood
.

Suppose instead that
q
does not lie in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
Since
\pi
is irreducible we deduce that the base-arc
I
for
q
is not the distinguished base-arc.
That is, we violate either the upper or lower base-arc inequality

1<\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}x_{\pi(k)}<1+\min\{x_{\pi(\ell)},x_{\pi(\ell+m)}\}.

We remedy this by applying the diagonal flow.
That is, there is some (non-unique) time
t(q)\in\mathbb{R}
such that
g_{t(q)}q
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.

Making choices, we have some admissible box neighbourhood
U(g_{t(q)}q)
.
In a slight abuse of notation, we take
U(q)=g_{-t(q)}U(g_{t(q)}q)
.
We call
U(q)
a
pre-admissible box neighbourhood
.
Therefore,
\partial U(q)
is a union of finitely many codimension-one embedded submanifolds (with boundary) in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.

The locus
\mathcal{V}
of saddled rooted differentials can be covered by countably many relatively open codimension-one charts.
Similarly, the locus
\mathcal{W}
of bi-saddled rooted differentials can be covered by countably many relatively open codimension-two charts.
Hence, we may apply a homotopy (relative to
q_{0}
) to arrange that
\gamma
is transverse to
\mathcal{V}
and to
\mathcal{W}

[
Hir94
, Theorem 2.5, page 78]
.
In particular, after this, the loop
\gamma
is disjoint from
\mathcal{W}
.
The boxes
(U({\gamma(s)}))_{s}
cover
\gamma
.
By compactness, there exists a finite collection
s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\in[0,1]
so that
(U({\gamma(s_{j})}))_{j}
covers
\gamma
.
Let
U_{j}=U(\gamma(s_{j}))
.
We add to the collection
(U_{j})
an admissible box neighbourhood
U_{0}
of
\gamma(s_{0})=q_{0}
.

Again appealing to
[
Hir94
, Theorem 2.5, page 78]
we perform a further homotopy (again relative to
q_{0}
) supported in the union of the boxes;
this makes
\gamma
transverse to the sides of the boxes
U_{j}
while keeping
\gamma
transverse to
\mathcal{V}
(and disjoint from
\mathcal{W}
).

This done,
\gamma
intersects
\partial U_{j}
only finitely many times.
Let
J_{0}=[0,s)\cup(s^{\prime},1]
be the union of the components of
\gamma^{-1}(U_{0})
containing the boundary of
I
.
Furthermore, for each
j>0
, the preimage
\gamma^{-1}(U_{j})
is a finite union of intervals in
[0,1]
.
All such intervals are (relatively) open.
We now select a minimal subcollection
J_{1},\dotsc,J_{m-1}
of these intervals that covers
[s,s^{\prime}]
;
we choose the indexing so that
J_{k}
is left of
J_{k+1}
.
We use
V_{k}
to denote the box
U_{j}
containing
\gamma(J_{k})
.
(Note that the list
V_{0},\dotsc,V_{m-1}
may contain repetitions.)
We now treat indices modulo
m
.
Thus, for all
k
, we have that
J_{k}\cap J_{k+1}
is a non-empty (relatively) open interval.
(There is a special case when
m=2
.)

Since
\gamma
is transverse to
\mathcal{V}
, we have that
\gamma(s)
lies in
\mathcal{V}
for at most countably many
s\in[0,1]
.
Thus, there exists a rooted differential
q_{k+1}
, without horizontal or vertical saddle connections, in the image of each set
\gamma(J_{k}\cap J_{k+1})
.
Hence, we obtain a sequence of times
0=s_{0}\leqslant s_{1}\leqslant\dotsb\leqslant s_{m}=1
such that the closed intervals
[s_{k},s_{k+1}]\subseteq[0,1]
cover
[0,1]
and
\gamma(s_{k})=q_{k}
.

Figure 4.58
.
Illustration of the proof of
Theorem

4.57
.
Part of the loop
\gamma
is depicted as a solid curve.
The dotted lines represent the boundaries of the polytopes.
Unlike the boxes
V_{k-1}
and
V_{k+1}
, the box
V_{k}
is not contained inside a polytope, so the diagonal flow must be applied to it.
The resulting segment
\delta_{k}
is shown as dashed curve.

Since
V_{k}
is a pre-admissible box neighbourhood there exists a real number
t_{k}
such that
g_{t_{k}}V_{k}
is completely contained inside some polytope in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
We now define a path
\delta_{k}
starting at
q_{k}
and ending at
q_{k+1}
.
If
t_{k}\geqslant 0
then
\delta_{k}
is the concatenation of

•

g_{t}q_{k}
for
t\in[0,t_{k}]
;

•

g_{t_{k}}\gamma(s)
for
s\in[s_{k},s_{k+1}]
; and

•

g_{-t}q_{k+1}
for
t\in[-t_{k},0]
.

If
t_{k}<0
then
\delta_{k}
is the concatenation of

•

g_{-t}q_{k}
for
t\in[0,-t_{k}]
;

•

g_{t_{k}}\gamma(s)
for
s\in[s_{k},s_{k+1}]
; and

•

g_{t}q_{k+1}
for
t\in[t_{k},0]
.

See
Figure

4.58
.

The union of
\delta_{k}
and
\gamma_{k}=\gamma|[s_{k},s_{k+1}]
bounds a disc in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
;
this disc is foliated by the arcs
g_{t}\gamma_{k}
where
t\in[0,t_{k}]
.
In particular,
\delta_{k}
and
\gamma_{k}
are homotopic, relative to their common endpoints.

Let
\delta
be the concatenation of the paths
\delta_{k}
.
By construction,
\delta
is a closed curve homotopic to
\gamma
, fixing
q_{0}
.
Moreover, the pieces
g_{t_{k}}\gamma(s)
for
s\in[s_{k},s_{k+1}]
in the concatenation are the only paths that are not (forward or backward) diagonal flow segments.
This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Remark 4.59
.

We do not attempt to make optimal choices to reduce the length of geodesic pieces in the concatenation for
\delta
.
A simple way to reduce these lengths is to choose the admissible zippered rectangle construction whenever
q
admits one.
Thus, if
q\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}-\mathcal{W}
is contained in some polytope, then we can choose the box
U(q)
to be contained inside the same polytope and set
t(q)=0
for such boxes.
On the other hand, when
q
does not lie in any polytope we can choose the length of the base-arc to be as close to
1
as possible, so
t(q)
is as small as possible.
See
Section

A.4
for a concrete example of the construction.
∎

4.60.
Rauzy–Veech induction

We now define Rauzy–Veech induction on zippered rectangles.
Let
I(r)
be the base-arc of a zippered rectangle decomposition of a rooted differential
(q,v)
.
Suppose that the underlying labelled permutation is
\pi
.
The induction is defined by passing to the longest base-arc
I(r^{\prime})
which is strictly contained in
I(r)
.
The base-arc
I(r^{\prime})
has length

|I(r^{\prime})|=|I(r)|-\min\{x_{\pi(\ell)},x_{\pi(\ell+m)}\}

We now say how the combinatorics and geometry of the decomposition change under a single Rauzy–Veech move.
Suppose that
\pi
is a labelled irreducible permutation.
Suppose that
(x,y)\in P(\pi)
are singularity parameters.

Let
\alpha=\pi(\ell)
and
\beta=\pi(\ell+m)
be the final letter on the top and bottom, respectively.
Since
\pi
is irreducible, we deduce that
\alpha\neq\beta
.
Suppose that
x_{\alpha}>x_{\beta}
.
Thus we say that the
top letter wins
.
The new parameters are

(4.61)

\displaystyle\begin{split}x^{\prime}_{\alpha}&=x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}\\
y^{\prime}_{\alpha}&=y_{\alpha}-y_{\beta}\end{split}

Also
x^{\prime}_{\rho}=x_{\rho}
for all
\rho\neq\alpha

and
y^{\prime}_{\rho}=y_{\rho}
for all
\rho\neq\beta
.

We encode the parameter transformations as a matrix.
Let
E=(e_{rs})
be the
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}
elementary matrix with ones along the diagonal, with
e_{\alpha\beta}=1
and with all other entries zero.

Convention 4.62
.

We write horizontal and vertical parameters as
column
vectors.
Thus
Ex^{\prime}=x
and
Ey^{\prime}=y
.
∎

Observe that our convention is
opposite
to the one used in Yoccoz’s lecture notes
[
Yoc10
, Section 3.4]
;
it allows us to avoid taking various transposes.

To define the new permutation we recall that
\alpha
is either a translation
or
flip letter.
Suppose
\alpha
is a translation letter.
Let
\pi(j)=\alpha
for some
\ell+1\leqslant j<\ell+m
.
We then set

•

\pi^{\prime}(i)=\pi(i)
for all
i\leqslant j
,

•

\pi^{\prime}(j+1)=\beta
, and

•

\pi^{\prime}(i)=\pi(i-1)
for all
i>j+1
.

Suppose instead that
\alpha
is a flip letter and let
\pi(j)=\alpha
for some
1\leqslant j<\ell
.
In
\pi^{\prime}
the top indices to range from
1
to
\ell+1
;
the bottom indices to range from
\ell+2
to
\ell+m
.
We then set

•

\pi^{\prime}(i)=\pi(i)
for all
i<j
,

•

\pi^{\prime}(j)=\beta
,

•

\pi^{\prime}(i)=\pi(i-1)
for all
i>j
.

With the above definitions, we set
R_{\mathrm{t}}(\pi,x,y)=(\pi^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
.
If
x_{\alpha}<x_{\beta}
instead, then we say that the
bottom letter wins
.
The definition of
R_{\mathrm{b}}
is very similar.

Remark 4.63
.

The induction is undefined on the codimension-one locus
x_{\alpha}=x_{\beta}
, which is contained in
\mathcal{V}
.
∎

The Rauzy–Veech moves give the labelled Rauzy class
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{lab}}
the structure of a directed graph.
We denote this graph by
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
.
The vertices of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
are irreducible labelled permutations in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{lab}}
.
We have an arrow from a permutation
\pi
to a permutation
\pi^{\prime}
if
\pi^{\prime}=R_{\mathrm{t}}(\pi)
or
R_{\mathrm{b}}(\pi)
.
A component of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
is called a
labelled Rauzy diagram
.

Suppose
\pi
and
\sigma
are permutations equivalent by the reindexing
s\in\Sym(\mathcal{A})
.
Then the permutations
\pi^{\prime}=R_{\mathrm{t}}(\pi)
and
