\sigma^{\prime}=R_{\mathrm{t}}(\sigma)
(or
R_{\mathrm{b}}(\pi)
and
R_{\mathrm{b}}(\sigma)
respectively) are also equivalent by
s
.
Thus, the Rauzy class
\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{root}}
inherits a directed graph structure from
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
.
This graph is denoted
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
: the
Rauzy diagram
.

Lemma 4.64
.

(Any component of)
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
is a regular cover of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are labelled generalised permutations in (a component of)
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
.
Suppose that
\pi^{\prime}
lies in the fibre of
[\pi]
.
That is, there is some reindexing
s\in\Sym(\mathcal{A})
so that
\pi^{\prime}=s\circ\pi
.
Since
s
gives a deck transformation of the covering
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}\to\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
(and so preserves components), we are done.
∎

We now explain the coding of the diagonal flow using admissible parameters and Rauzy–Veech induction.
Suppose that
\pi
is a labelled irreducible generalised permutation.
Let
\mathcal{C}(\pi)=q_{\pi}(P(\pi))
be the corresponding polytope.
Let
\alpha=\pi(\ell)
and
\beta=\pi(\ell+m)
be the final letters on the top and the bottom.
Suppose that
(x,y)
lies in
P(\pi)
.
Thus
q=q_{\pi}(x,y)
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
Suppose that
I=I(r)
is the distinguished base-arc for
q
.
Let
(t_{-},t_{+})=(t_{-}(q),t_{+}(q))
be the largest interval so that
for all
t\in(t_{-},t_{+})
we have
g_{t}(x,y)
lying in
P(\pi)
.
Note that
t_{-}<0<t_{+}
because
I
is the distinguished base-arc.

The parameters
g_{t_{-}}(x,y)
and
g_{t_{+}}(x,y)
satisfy all conditions of
4.50

except
the lower or upper bounds in the base-arc inequalities, respectively.
We define
q_{-}=g_{t_{-}}q
and
q_{+}=g_{t_{+}}q
.
By
[
BL09
, Lemma 2.12]
, the parameters
g_{t_{-}}(x,y)
and
g_{t_{+}}(x,y)
give zippered rectangles decompositions of
q_{-}
and
q_{+}
respectively.
It also follows that

•

t_{-}=-\log|I|
and
I
gives a base-arc
I_{-}
in
q_{-}
with width one;

•

t_{+}=-\log(|I|-\min\{x_{\alpha},x_{\beta}\})
and
I
gives a base-arc
I_{+}
in
q_{+}
whose width equals the upper bound in the base-arc inequalities.

We call
g_{t_{-}}(x,y)
a
backwards almost admissible parameter
for
\pi
and we call
q_{-}
a
backwards almost admissible differential
for
\pi
.
We use similar language in the forwards direction.

Definition 4.65
.

The subset
\partial^{-}P(\pi)
of backwards almost admissible parameters is the
backwards flow face
of
P(\pi)
.
We make similar definitions for
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}(\pi)
as well as
\partial^{+}P(\pi)
and
\partial^{+}\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
∎

Notation 4.66
.

We take
\partial^{-}P=\bigsqcup_{\pi}\partial^{-}P(\pi)
and
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}=\bigsqcup_{\pi}\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
∎

Lemma 4.67
.

Suppose that the rooted differential
(q,v)
lies in
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
Then there is some
\delta>0
so that:

(1-\delta,1+\delta)\cap E(q,v)=\{1\}

Proof.

By definition,
1
lies in
E(q,v)
and there is some
\epsilon>0
(depending on
(q,v)
) so that

•

g_{\epsilon}q
is contained in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
and

•

the geodesic segment
[q,g_{\epsilon}q]
meets
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}
in exactly one point (that is, at
q
).

By
4.45
, the set
E(g_{\epsilon}q,v)
contains some
s<1
.
Hence,
E(q,v)
contains
e^{-\epsilon}s
.
We conclude the proof by
Lemma

4.10
.
∎

The length of the base-arc
I
is the sum of the widths.
Therefore,
\partial^{-}P(\pi)
has codimension one in
P(\pi)
.
Similarly,
\partial^{+}P(\pi)
is contained in the union of two codimension-one loci.

Note that it is possible for
\partial^{+}\mathcal{C}(\pi)
and
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}(\pi)
to intersect.
This happens if and only if there is a Rauzy–Veech move from
\pi
to itself.
In particular, if
R_{\mathrm{t}}(\pi)=\pi
, then
\pi(\ell+m-1)=\alpha
.
Finally, if
R_{\mathrm{b}}(\pi)=\pi
, then
\pi(\ell-1)=\beta
.

The above discussion gives the following lemma.

Lemma 4.68
.

The map
q_{\pi}\colon P(\pi)\to\mathcal{C}(\pi)
extends continuously to
a homeomorphism

q_{\pi}^{-}\colon P(\pi)\cup\partial^{-}P(\pi)\to\mathcal{C}(\pi)\cup\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}(\pi)

Similarly, it extends continuously to a homeomorphism

q_{\pi}^{+}\colon P(\pi)\cup\partial^{+}P(\pi)\to\mathcal{C}(\pi)\cup\partial^{+}\mathcal{C}(\pi)

These maps conjugate the diagonal flow on parameters to the diagonal flow on differentials. ∎

Remark 4.69
.

There is also a continuous extension to
q_{\pi}^{\pm}\colon P(\pi)\cup\partial^{\pm}P(\pi)
.
However, this extension need not be a homeomorphism.
See
Section

A.4
.
∎

Remark 4.70
.

Strictly speaking, we must define and give notation to the resulting quotient; that is, identifying points in
P\cup\partial^{\pm}P
having the same image under
\bigsqcup q_{\pi}^{\pm}
.
As we shall see, the diagonal flow
g_{t}
is well-defined on all but a measure zero subset of this quotient.
Also
\bigsqcup q_{\pi}
is a measurable conjugacy between the diagonal flows on parameters and on differentials.

However, we will instead slightly abuse notation and use
P\cup\partial^{-}P
to denote this quotient.
∎

Let
\partial^{+}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{t}}(\pi)
be the subset
\partial^{+}\mathcal{C}(\pi)\cap q_{\pi}(\{(x,y):x_{\alpha}>x_{\beta}\})
.
Similarly, let
\partial^{+}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{b}}(\pi)
be the subset
\partial^{+}\mathcal{C}(\pi)\cap q_{\pi}(\{(x,y):x_{\beta}>x_{\alpha}\})
.

Lemma 4.71
.

Let
q
be a rooted differential in
\partial^{+}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{t}}(\pi)
(respectively
\partial^{+}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{b}}(\pi)
).
Then
q
is contained in
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}(R_{\mathrm{t}}(\pi))
(respectively
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}(R_{\mathrm{b}}(\pi))
).

Proof.

Suppose that
q
lies in
\partial^{+}\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
By
Lemma

4.68
there are parameters
(x,y)
in the upper boundary of
P(\pi)
so that
q=q_{\pi}(x,y)
.
Let
I
be the associated base-arc for
q
.
Breaking symmetry, suppose that
x_{\alpha}>x_{\beta}
.
(That is,
q
lies in
\partial^{+}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{t}}(\pi)
.)
Thus
|I|=1+x_{\beta}
.
By
Equation

4.61
we can perform a Rauzy–Veech move and obtain
R_{\mathrm{t}}(\pi,x,y)=(\pi^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
.
The width of the base-arc
I^{\prime}
after the move is
|I|-x_{\beta}=1
.
Thus
q
lies in
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}(\pi^{\prime})=\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}(R_{\mathrm{t}}(\pi))
, as desired.
∎

4.72.
The diagonal flow and Rauzy-Veech induction

Suppose that
q
is contained in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
with the distinguished base-arc
I
.
The rooted differential
q_{+}=g_{t_{+}}q
lies in
\partial^{+}\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
Suppose that the parameters for
q
, hence for
q_{+}
, satisfy
x_{\alpha}\neq x_{\beta}
.
By
Lemma

4.71
, the roote differential
q_{+}
is contained in
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}(\pi^{\prime})
where
\pi^{\prime}=R_{\ast}(\pi)
(here
\ast=\mathrm{t}
or
\ast=\mathrm{b}
depending on which of
x_{\alpha}
,
x_{\beta}
is larger).
We repeat the process by flowing
q_{+}
forward to get a rooted differential
q^{\prime}_{+}
in
\partial^{+}\mathcal{C}(\pi^{\prime})
.
Let
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
be the top and bottom rightmost letters in
\pi^{\prime}
.
If the
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}
widths in
q^{\prime}_{+}
are not equal then we may define
\pi^{\prime\prime}=R_{\ast}(\pi^{\prime})
, we see that
q^{\prime}_{+}
lies in
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}(\pi^{\prime\prime})
, and we may continue to iterate.
The iteration stops (after finitely many Rauzy–Veech moves) if and only if
q
has a vertical saddle connection
[
BL09
, Proposition 4.2]
.

Since
\mathcal{C}(\pi)=\mathcal{C}(\sigma)
for equivalent permutations, the quotient graph, namely the Rauzy diagram
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
, codes the diagonal flow on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.

Remark 4.73
.

As recalled in
Appendix

D
, the Masur–Smillie–Veech measure is a diagonal flow-invariant measure that is ergodic.
By ergodicity, a set of positive measure in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)-\mathcal{V}
, visits every
\mathcal{C}(\sigma)
under the diagonal flow.
This implies that the Rauzy diagram
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
is
strongly connected
:
there is a directed path between any pair of vertices.
Since each component of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
evenly covers
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
, the components of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
are also strongly connected (as noted by Boissy–Lanneau
[
BL09
]
).
∎

We fix a component of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
for the rest of the article.
With a slight abuse of notation, we (again) simply call this component
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
.

The lemmas below are standard facts in Rauzy–Veech theory and are often implicitly used.
We include proof sketches for completeness.
Recall from
4.5
that
E(q,v)
is the set of endpoints of base-arcs.

Lemma 4.74
.

Suppose that
\pi
is a labelled permutation in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
.
Suppose that
(q,v)
is a rooted differential in the polytope
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
Let
T_{0}=-\log(\inf E(q,v))
.
(If the infimum is zero, we take
T_{0}=\infty
.)
Then, for any positive
T<T_{0}
, the diagonal flow segment
[q,g_{T}q]
crosses only finitely many flow faces.

Proof.

Let
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}_{1},\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}_{2},\dots
be the sequence of backwards flow faces crossed by the segment
[q,g_{T}q]
.
Let
0<t_{k}\leqslant T
be the monotonically increasing sequence of times such that
g_{t_{k}}q
is contained in
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}_{k}
.
Let
I_{v}
be the horizontal separatrix emanating from the root.
It follows that the sequence of points
e^{-t_{k}}
in
I_{v}
give base-arcs for
q
.
Thus they are all contained in
E(q,v)
.

Since
T<T_{0}
, by
Lemma

4.10
the intersection
E(q,v)\cap[e^{-T},1)
is finite.
Hence, the sequence
t_{k}
is finite and we are done.
∎

Remark 4.75
.

Recall that the differentials
q_{k}
introduced in the proof of
Theorem

4.57
did not have any vertical or horizontal saddle connections.
Thus by
Lemma

4.10
the geodesic segments
\delta_{k}
(also introduced in the proof) satisfy the hypothesis of
Lemma

4.74
.
Thus the
\delta_{k}
cross only finitely many flow faces.
∎

Definition 4.76
.

A
Rauzy–Veech sequence
is a concatenation of Rauzy–Veech moves labelling a directed path in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
.
∎

Let
\zeta
be a finite Rauzy–Veech sequence that starts and ends at labelled permutations
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
.
Let
P(\zeta)\subseteq P(\pi)
be the parameters whose Rauzy–Veech sequence begins with
\zeta
.
By induction on the number of moves, we conclude that
P(\zeta)
is a convex open subset of
P(\pi)
.
In particular,
\mathcal{C}(\zeta)=q_{\pi}(P(\zeta))
is path connected.

Definition 4.77
.

Suppose that
q
is a differential and
t
is a time.
Suppose that
q
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
and
q^{\prime}=g_{t}q
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\pi^{\prime})
.
We say that the geodesic segment
[q,q^{\prime}]
is a
\zeta
–segment
if the Rauzy–Veech sequence of
[q,g_{t}q]
from
\pi
is
\zeta
.
∎

It follows that
q\in\mathcal{C}(\zeta)
.
We shall see (in
4.83
) that every finite Rauzy–Veech sequence
\zeta
admits
\zeta
–segments.

Remark 4.78
.

Suppose that
q
is a differential and
t
is a time.
Suppose that
q
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)\cup\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}(\pi)
and
q^{\prime}=g_{t}q
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\pi^{\prime})\cup\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}(\pi^{\prime})
.
Then, by
4.65
, there is a small
\epsilon>0
(depending on
q
and
q^{\prime}
) so that

•

g_{\epsilon}q
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
,

•

g_{\epsilon}q^{\prime}
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\pi^{\prime})
, and

•

the interiors of the segments
[q,g_{\epsilon}q]
and
[q^{\prime},g_{\epsilon}q^{\prime}]
do not cross flow faces.

We say that the geodesic segment
[q,q^{\prime}]

\zeta
–segment
if
[g_{\epsilon}q,g_{\epsilon}q^{\prime}]
is.
∎

Lemma 4.79
.

Suppose that
\zeta=\zeta^{\prime}\zeta^{\prime\prime}
is a concatenation of finite Rauzy–Veech sequences.
Suppose that
[q,g_{t}q]
is a
\zeta
–segment.
Then there is a time
s\in[0,t]
so that
[q,g_{s}q]
is a
\zeta^{\prime}
–segment and
[g_{s}q,g_{t}q]
is a
\zeta^{\prime\prime}
–segment.

Proof.

As in
4.78
we may (if needed) move the endpoints of the segment slightly to ensure that the endpoints lie in the interiors of polytopes.

Note that, By definition,
P(\zeta)
is a subset of
P(\zeta^{\prime})
.
Let
q=q_{\pi}(x,y)
.
Then
(x,y)
lies in
P(\zeta)
, hence in
P(\zeta^{\prime})
.
Thus there is a time
s\leqslant t
such that
[q,g_{s}q]
is a
\zeta^{\prime}
–segment.
Suppose that
\zeta^{\prime}
ends at the labelled permutation
\pi^{\prime}
.
Since
[q,g_{s}q]
can be extended by
\zeta^{\prime\prime}
to a
\zeta
–segment, it follows that
[g_{s}q,g_{t}q]
is a
\zeta^{\prime\prime}
–segment.
∎

The segments associated with finite Rauzy-Veech sequences are “unique up to homotopy”, as follows.

Lemma 4.80
.

Suppose that
\zeta
is a finite Rauzy–Veech sequence.
Then any pair of
\zeta
–segments are isotopic in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
through
\zeta
–segments.

Proof.

Suppose that
\zeta
starts and ends at labelled permutations
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
.
Let
[p,g_{t}p]
and
[q,g_{t^{\prime}}q]
be
\zeta
–segments.
Applying
4.78
we may assume that all endpoints lie in the interiors of polytopes.
Thus
p
and
q
lie in
\mathcal{C}(\zeta)
, the image of
P(\zeta)
.
Suppose that
\rho\colon[0,1]\to P(\zeta)
is a line segment connecting the parameters of
p
to those of
q
.
Then for any
r\in[0,1]
we have that
q_{\pi}(\rho(r))
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\zeta)
.
Note that
q_{\pi}(\rho(0))=p
and
q_{\pi}(\rho(1))=q
.
Set
q_{r}=q_{\pi}(\rho(r))
.
We choose times
t(r)
continuously so that
t(0)=t
, so that
t(1)=t^{\prime}
, and so that
[q_{r},g_{t(r)}q_{r}]
is a
\zeta
–segment.
This gives the required isotopy.
∎

We give a full exposition of the
width
and
height cones
in
Appendix

D
, starting from
D.3
.
Suppose that
\zeta
is a finite Rauzy–Veech sequence from
\pi
to
\pi^{\prime}
.
We define
X(\zeta)
to be the cone of widths in
X(\pi)
whose Rauzy–Veech sequence is given by
\zeta
.
Note that
X(\zeta)
is the cone over the width parameters of points in
P(\zeta)
.
After recalling
Equation

4.61
and
4.62
, we induct on the length of
\zeta
to obtain a matrix
E_{\zeta}=E_{1}\cdots E_{n}
.
Here
E_{i}
is the elementary matrix for the
i^{\text{th}}
Rauzy–Veech move in
\zeta
.
If
(x,y)
lies in
P(\zeta)
, then the new widths and heights
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
lie in
P(\pi^{\prime})
.
The new widths and the old are related as follows.

(4.81)

(E_{\zeta}x^{\prime},E_{\zeta}y^{\prime})=(x,y)

This extends
Equation

4.61
.
In particular, we have

(4.82)

X(\zeta)=E_{\zeta}(X(\pi^{\prime}))\subseteq X(\pi)

Remark 4.83
.

Note that
X(\zeta)
is nonempty.
Thus any finite Rauzy–Veech sequence
\zeta
admits
\zeta
–segments.
∎

Lemma 4.84
.

Suppose that
\pi
is a labelled permutation.
Suppose that
U
is an open subset of
P(\pi)
.
Then there is a finite Rauzy–Veech sequence
\theta
, starting from
\pi
, so that

\overline{X(\theta)}-\{0\}\subseteq X(U)

The same holds for any extension
\theta\eta
of
\theta
.

We also give a version of this for the height cones in
Lemma

5.12
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.84
.

By Masur
[
Mas82
]
and Veech
[
Vee82
]
(more strongly
by Kerckhoff, Masur, and Smillie
[
KMS86
]
, see also
[
Yoc06
, Section 4.4, Theorem]
), a typical differential (with respect to the Masur–Smillie–Veech measure - see
Appendix

D
) has a uniquely ergodic vertical foliation.
Suppose that
q\in q_{\pi}(U)
is one such.
Let
(x,y)\in P(\pi)
be the parameters for
q
.
By definition,
x
lies in
X(U)
.
Also,
q
admits an infinite Rauzy–Veech sequence
\zeta
.
Let
\zeta_{n}
be its prefix of length
n
.
Let
E_{n}
be the Rauzy matrix of
\zeta_{n}
.
Suppose that
\zeta_{n}
ends at the labelled permutation
\pi_{n}
.
Note that
X(\zeta_{n})=E_{n}X(\pi_{n})
gives a sequence of nested cones in
X(\pi)
.
By unique ergodicity, the intersection of the closures of these cones is exactly the ray through
x
.
(In the abelian case this is
[
Yoc06
, Section 4.4, Proposition]
;
the same proof works in the quadratic case.)
Since
U
is open, for some sufficiently large
n
we have that
\overline{X(\zeta_{n})}-\{0\}
is contained inside
X(U)
.
Setting
\theta
to be
\zeta_{n}
, we are done.
∎

By
Lemma

4.84
, it follows that if
q
lies in
\mathcal{C}(\theta)
then the maximal geodesic segment in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
containing
q
intersects
q_{\pi}(U)
.

5.
The flow group is the fundamental group

5.1.
Fundamental groups of directed graphs

We will need the following lemma.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{D}
is a directed graph which is strongly connected.
Suppose that
\pi
is a vertex of
\mathcal{D}
.
Then
\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{D},\pi)
is generated by the homotopy classes of directed loops based at
\pi
. ∎

Remark 5.3
.

If
\mathcal{D}
is finite then finitely many directed loops suffice.
∎

5.4.
Rauzy diagrams and the flow group

Suppose that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
is a Rauzy diagram associated to stratum component of rooted differentials
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
Let
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
be (a component of) the labelled Rauzy diagram.

For every vertex
\pi
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
we choose a base-point
q^{\pi}
in the interior of
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
For every directed edge
e=(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
we choose a base-point
q^{e}
in
\partial^{+}\mathcal{C}(\pi)\cap\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}(\pi^{\prime})
: the intersection of the forwards and backwards flow faces.
For example, we could produce such
q^{e}
using an
e
–segment.

Suppose that
s\in\Sym(\mathcal{A})
is a reindexing.
By
Lemma

4.46
, we may choose base-points in polytopes to satisfy
q^{\pi}=q^{s\circ\pi}
.
By
Lemma

4.64
,
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is an edge if and only if
(s\circ\pi,s\circ\pi^{\prime})
is an edge.
Hence, we may choose base-points on flow faces to satisfy
q^{e}=q^{e^{\prime}}
if and only if
e
and
e^{\prime}
are equivalent by reindexing.

By connecting the base-points according to the combinatorics of Rauzy–Veech moves, we build a graph
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{root}}
that is isomorphic to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
We choose a homeomorphism from
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
to
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{root}}
which (for all
\pi
and
e
) sends
\pi
to
q^{\pi}
and sends the midpoint of
e
to
q^{e}
.

Fix a base-point
[\pi]
for
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
The homeomorphism from
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
to
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{root}}
induces a homomorphism

(5.5)

\Psi\colon\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}},[\pi])\to\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}},q^{\pi})

Here is our first consequence of
Theorem

4.57
.

Theorem 5.6
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
is a component of a stratum of rooted abelian or quadratic differentials. Suppose that
\pi
a labelled permutation in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
.
Then the homomorphism
\Psi
(given in
Equation

5.5
) is surjective.

Proof.

Suppose that
\gamma
is a loop in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
based at
q^{\pi}
.
By
Theorem

4.57
, we can homotope
\gamma
(relative to its base-point) to a finite concatenation of paths
\gamma_{i}
where each
\gamma_{i}
is either a (forwards or backwards) geodesic segment or is contained inside a polytope.
Breaking symmetry, we assume
\gamma
is the concatenation
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\dotsb\gamma_{k}
where the odd indexed
\gamma_{i}
are contained in a polytope and the even indexed
\gamma_{i}
are (forwards or backwards) geodesic segments.

Applying
4.75
, we have finite Rauzy–Veech sequences
\zeta_{2i}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
as follows:

•

traversed forward, each
\gamma_{2i}
is a
\zeta_{2i}
–segment;

•

\zeta_{2}
starts at
\pi
; and

•

the end of
\zeta_{2i}
is the beginning of
\zeta_{2i+2}
.

Each
\zeta_{2i}
descends to a path
\kappa_{2i}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
By
Lemma

4.79
, the geodesic
\gamma_{2i}
visits (in the correct order) the polytopes coded by
\zeta_{2i}
.
Since the
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
are polytopes (and the same essentially holds for the flow faces) we can homotope
\gamma_{2i}
to move its intersections with the flow faces to the points
q^{e}
.
This done we may homotope each
\gamma_{2i+1}
to the corresponding point
q^{\pi}
and each
\gamma_{2i}
(relative to the points
q^{e}
) to (the image in the graph
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{root}}
of) the corresponding path
\kappa_{2i}
.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

The components of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
evenly cover
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
Thus, by
Theorem

5.6
, corresponding to each component of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
there is a finite cover
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{lab}}
of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
This cover has an intrinsic description for abelian components (see
[
Boi15
, Proposition 1.2]
).
It is an open problem to give an intrinsic description of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{lab}}
in the quadratic case (see
[
Boi15
, Appendix A]
).
∎

Suppose that
q_{0}
is a base-point in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
Suppose that
U
is a contractible open set around
q_{0}
.
For every
q\in U
, we choose a path
\eta_{q}
from
q_{0}
to
q
inside of
U
.
As
U
is contractible, any choice of
\eta_{q}
is homotopic to any other relative to the end-points.
By
\overline{\eta}_{q}
, we mean
\eta_{q}
traversed in reverse.

Suppose that
\gamma
is a geodesic segment whose endpoints
q
and
q^{\prime}
lie in
U
.
The concatenation
\eta_{q}\gamma\overline{\eta}_{q^{\prime}}
is a loop in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
based at
q_{0}
.
We call such loops
almost-flow loops
based at
(U,q_{0})
.

Definition 5.8
.

The flow group
\mathrm{Flow}(U,q_{0})
is the subgroup of
\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}},q_{0})
generated by the almost-flow loops based at
(U,q_{0})
.
∎

Here is one of our main results.

Theorem 5.9
.

Suppose that
U
is a contractible open set in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
Suppose that
q_{0}
is a differential in
U
.
Then

\mathrm{Flow}(U,q_{0})=\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}},q_{0})

Proof.

By
Lemma

4.48
, the union of the polytopes
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
is dense in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
Thus, applying the change of base-point isomorphism for
\uppi_{1}
, we may assume that
q_{0}
lies in some
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
Note that if
V\subseteq U
also contains
q_{0}
then
\mathrm{Flow}(V,q_{0})\leqslant\mathrm{Flow}(U,q_{0})
.
Thus we may assume that
U
is contained in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
Applying
Lemma

5.2
and
Theorem

5.6
, it suffices to show that for any directed based loop
\zeta
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
there is a product of almost flow loops based at
(U,q_{0})
whose Rauzy–Veech sequences concatenate give the unique lift of
\zeta
to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
starting at
\pi
.

By
Lemma

4.84
, there is a finite Rauzy–Veech sequence
\theta
starting at
\pi
so that

\overline{X(\theta)}\subseteq X(q_{\pi}^{-1}(U))
.
It follows that the same is true for any finite extension of
\theta
.
So we may use strong connectivity of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
to extend
\theta
until it ends at
\pi
.
Thus
\theta
is a loop in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
.
Thus
\theta
also gives a loop in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
– in a small abuse of notation we use the same name for both.

We now must force a geodesic segment to meet
U
twice.
Suppose that
q
lies in the intersection of
\mathcal{C}(\theta^{2})
and
U
.
Let
[q,g_{b}q]
be a
\theta^{2}
–segment.
By
Lemma

4.79
, there is a time
a\in(0,b)
as follows:

•

[q,g_{a}q]
and
[g_{a}q,g_{b}q]
are both
\theta
–segments and

•

g_{a}q
is contained in
\mathcal{C}(\theta)\cap U
.

Thus
\gamma_{\theta}=[q,g_{a}q]
begins and ends in
U
.
The resulting almost-flow loop
\eta_{q}\gamma_{\theta}\overline{\eta}_{g_{a}q}
lies in the based homotopy class
\Psi([\theta])
(with
\Psi
as defined in
Equation

5.5
).

Suppose that
\zeta
is any directed based loop in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
The concatenation
\theta\zeta\theta
is a directed based loop in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
Suppose that this lifts to the Rauzy–Veech sequence
\xi
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
, starting from the labelled permutation
\pi
.

Since
\xi
extends
\theta
, there is a differential
q
in the intersection of
\mathcal{C}(\xi)
and
U
.
Let
\gamma_{\xi}=[q,g_{d}q]
be a
\xi
–segment.
By
Lemma

4.79
, there is a time
c\in(0,d)
as follows:

•

[q,g_{c}q]
is a
\theta\zeta
–segment,

•

[g_{c}q,g_{d}q]
is a
\theta
–segment, and

•

g_{c}q
is contained in the intersection
\mathcal{C}(\theta)\cap U
.

Thus
\gamma_{\theta\zeta}=[q,g_{c}q]
begins and ends in
U
.
The resulting almost-flow loop
\eta_{q}\gamma_{\theta\zeta}\overline{\eta}_{g_{c}q}
lies in the based homotopy class
\Psi([\theta\zeta])
.

Since
\mathrm{Flow}(U,q_{0})
is a subgroup we deduce that there is a product giving
\Psi([\zeta])
, as desired.
∎

We also obtain a “flow loop” version of
Theorem

5.9
.
Suppose that
q_{0}
is a base-point in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
Suppose that
U
is a contractible open set around
q_{0}
.

Definition 5.10
.

The
strict flow group

\mathrm{SFlow}(U,q_{0})
is the subgroup of
\mathrm{Flow}(U,q_{0})
generated by loops of the form
\eta_{q}\gamma\overline{\eta}_{q}
where

•

\gamma
is a closed geodesic that intersects
U
and

•

q
is any point of
U\cap\gamma
. ∎

Recall that
Y(\pi)
is the open cone of height parameters for
\pi
(
D.3
).
Recall that
P(\pi)
is the polytope of parameters for
\pi
.
For any open set
U
in
P(\pi)
, we define
Y(U)
to be the cone over the height parameters of points in
U
.

Suppose that
\zeta
is a finite Rauzy–Veech sequence from
\pi
to
\pi^{\prime}
.
We define

(5.11)

Y(\zeta)=E_{\zeta}^{-1}(Y(\pi))\subseteq Y(\pi^{\prime})

Recall that widths and heights are, respectively, unstable and stable for the flow.
This is why when we go from
Equation

4.82
to
Equation

5.11
we must replace
E_{\zeta}
by
E_{\zeta}^{-1}
.

Lemma 5.12
.

Suppose that
\pi
is a labelled permutation.
Suppose that
U
is an open subset of
P(\pi)
.
Then there is a finite Rauzy–Veech sequence
\theta
, ending at
\pi
, so that

\overline{Y(\theta)}-\{0\}\subseteq Y(U)

The same holds for any extension
\eta\theta
of
\theta
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of
Lemma

4.84
but instead using the genericity of uniquely ergodic horizontal foliations.
∎

Definition 5.13
.

Suppose that
U
is an open set contained in a polytope
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
We call a based (at
\pi
) Rauzy–Veech loop
\theta

strongly nested for
U
if

•

\overline{X(\theta)}-\{0\}
is contained in
X(q_{\pi}^{-1}(U))
; and

•

\overline{Y(\theta)}-\{0\}
is contained in
Y(q_{\pi}^{-1}(U))
.

∎

Lemma 5.14
.

Suppose that
U
is an open set contained in
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.
Then there is a Rauzy–Veech loop
\theta
, based at
\pi
, that is strongly nested for
U
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

4.84
, there is a based (at
\pi
) Rauzy–Veech loop
\zeta
such that
\overline{X(\zeta)}-\{0\}
is contained in
X(q_{\pi}^{-1}(U))
.
By
Lemma

5.12
, there is a based (also at
\pi
) Rauzy–Veech loop
\zeta^{\prime}
such that
\overline{Y(\zeta^{\prime})}-\{0\}
is contained in
Y(q_{\pi}^{-1}(U))
.
We deduce that the concatenation
\zeta\zeta^{\prime}
is strongly nested for
U
.
∎

When
X(q_{\pi}^{-1}(U))=X(\pi)
then positivity of the Rauzy matrix implies (but is not implied by) nesting in the widths.
For abelian strata, Marmi–Moussa–Yoccoz produce a sequence
\zeta
so that the matrix
E_{\zeta}
is positive; see
[
Yoc06
, Proposition 2]
.
By extending it, we may assume
\zeta
is a based loop.
The same proof works for quadratic strata.
For related discussion see
[
AR12
, Lemma 3.12]
.
This gives the following.

Corollary 5.15
.

Suppose that
U\subseteq\mathcal{C}(\pi)
is an open subset.
Then there is a based loop
\theta
which is strongly nested for
U
and so that its Rauzy matrix
E_{\theta}
is positive.

Proof.

Let
\zeta
be a based loop so that the matrix
E_{\zeta}
is positive.
By
Lemma

5.14
, there is a Rauzy–Veech loop
\eta
, based at
\pi
, that is strongly nesting for
U
.
The based loop
\theta=\eta\zeta\eta
is thus strongly nesting for
U
and has a positive Rauzy matrix.
∎

Strong nesting using a sequence with a positive Rauzy matrix simplifies the discussion in
Section

6
.

Corollary 5.16
.

Suppose that
q_{0}
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
Suppose that
U
is a contractible open set containing
q_{0}
.
Then

\mathrm{SFlow}(U,q_{0})=\uppi_{1}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}},q_{0}).

Proof.

By
Theorem

5.9
, it suffices to prove that
\mathrm{SFlow}(U,q_{0})=\mathrm{Flow}(U,q_{0})
. As before, we may (and do) assume that
U
is contained in some polytope
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
.

Suppose that
\gamma
is a geodesic segment that starts at
q
and ends at
q^{\prime}
in
U
.
So
\eta_{q}\gamma\overline{\eta}_{q^{\prime}}
is an almost flow loop.
Since
\gamma
returns to
\mathcal{C}(\pi)
we deduce that
\gamma
is a
\zeta
–segment, for some based loop
\zeta
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
starting from
\pi
.

Let
\theta
be a Rauzy–Veech loop, based at
\pi
, that is strongly nested for
U
.
Suppose that
\xi
is any other Rauzy–Veech loop, based at
\pi
(perhaps of length zero).
Then the extension
\theta\xi
satisfies

•

the sequence
X((\theta\xi)^{n})
converges as
n\to\infty
to a ray in
X(q_{\pi}^{-1}(U))
and

•

the sequence
Y((\theta\xi)^{n})
converges as
n\to\infty
to a ray in
Y(q_{\pi}^{-1}(U))
.

Let
x^{\theta\xi}
and
y^{\theta\xi}
be parameters that span the rays.
Then, after appropriate scalings,
we have that
q^{\theta\xi}=q_{\pi}(x^{\theta\xi},y^{\theta\xi})
lies in
U
.
Let
\gamma^{\theta}
and
\gamma^{\theta\xi}
be the orbits of
q^{\theta}
and
\gamma^{\theta\xi}
, respectively, under the diagonal flow.
Then:

•

\gamma^{\theta}
and
\gamma^{\theta\xi}
are closed geodesics and

•

the Rauzy–Veech sequences have, respectively, period
\theta
and
\theta\xi
.

We use
p^{\prime}
to denote
q^{\theta}
and
p^{\prime\prime}
to denote
q^{\theta\zeta}
.
Now consider the closed geodesics
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma^{\theta}
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}=\gamma^{\theta\zeta}
.
The based homotopy classes of
\eta_{p^{\prime}}\gamma^{\prime}\overline{\eta}_{p^{\prime}}
and
\eta_{p^{\prime\prime}}\gamma^{\prime\prime}\overline{\eta}_{p^{\prime\prime}}
are generators of
\mathrm{SFlow}(U,q_{0})
.
Also,
Lemma

4.80
, the original almost flow loop
\eta_{q}\gamma\overline{\eta}_{q^{\prime}}

is homotopic to

\overline{\eta_{p^{\prime}}\gamma^{\prime}\overline{\eta}_{p^{\prime}}}\cdot\eta_{p^{\prime\prime}}\gamma^{\prime\prime}\overline{\eta}_{p^{\prime\prime}}

Thus the class of
\eta_{q}\gamma\overline{\eta}_{q^{\prime}}
belongs to
\mathrm{SFlow}(U,q_{0})
, as desired.
∎

6.
Dynamics of the diagonal flow

6.1.
Coding formalism

Let
\Pi
be a finite or countable alphabet.
We consider the symbolic space
\Sigma=\Pi^{\mathbb{Z}}
endowed with the left shift map
S
.
Suppose that
u\in\Pi^{m}
and
v\in\Pi^{n}
are finite words.
We write
uv\in\Pi^{m+n}
for their concatenation.
The (forward) cylinder
\Sigma(u)
induced by
u
is defined as

\Sigma(u)=\{a\in\Sigma\mid a_{k}=u_{k}\text{ for }0\leqslant k<m\}

Definition 6.2
.

Suppose that
\mu
is an
S
–invariant finite measure on
\Sigma
.
We say that
\mu
has
bounded distortion
if there exists a constant
M\geqslant 1
such that, for any finite words
u\in\Pi^{r}
and
v\in\Pi^{s}
,

\frac{1}{M}\mu(\Sigma(uv))\leqslant\mu(\Sigma(u))\mu(\Sigma(v))\leqslant M\mu(\Sigma(uv))\qed

This generalises the property of being a
Bernoulli shift
, where
M=1
.
For this reason, it is also called an
approximate product structure
.

6.3.
A smaller section for the Rauzy–Veech renormalisation

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
is a component of rooted differentials from a stratum component
\mathcal{C}
.
We take
\mathcal{C}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{root}}
to be the locus of rooted differentials of area one.
Note that this is preserved by the diagonal flow.

Notation 6.4
.

Suppose that
U
is a subset of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
closed under scaling: that is,
\mathbb{R}\cdot U=U
.
Then we set
U^{(1)}=U\cap\mathcal{C}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
We use similar notation in the parameter space.
∎

We now turn to the task of building a coding for the system
(\mathcal{C}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{root}},g_{t})
.
The natural alphabet is in bijection with single moves in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
(and so is finite).
The corresponding Poincaré section is

\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{root}}=\bigsqcup_{[\pi]}\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}^{(1)}(\pi)

As discussed in
Section

D.9
(see in particular
Lemma

D.11
) this section carries the measure
\nu^{(1)}
which is invariant for the Rauzy–Veech renormalisation.

However, there are two problems with this choice of alphabet and section.

•

Every backwards flow face
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}^{(1)}(\pi)
has infinite
\nu^{(1)}
–mass. See
[
Vee82
]
for a proof in the abelian case;
a similar calculation holds in the quadratic case.

•

The measure
\nu^{(1)}
does not have bounded distortion for finite Rauzy–Veech sequences.
This is a consequence of
[
Vee78
, Proposition 5.2]
in the abelian case and of
[
Gad12
, Equation 8.3 and Lemma 8.5]
in the quadratic case.

These imply, respectively, that the existence of the Lyapunov spectrum is unclear and that we cannot apply the simplicity criterion of Avila–Viana
[
AV07
]
.

In the abelian case, Zorich
[
Zor97
]
gives an acceleration of the Rauzy–Veech renormalisation.
The result is a larger (now countable) alphabet but a smaller Poincaré section with finite
\nu^{(1)}
–mass.
This deals with the first problem, but not the second.
To control the distortion, again in the abelian case, Avila–Gouëzel–Yoccoz give a further acceleration that results in a pre-compact (in
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}^{(1)}
) Poincaré section.

Here we extend their technique to deal with quadratic strata.
Suppose that
\pi
is a labelled irreducible generalised permutation.
Define
X_{1}(\pi)=\{x\in X(\pi)\mid w_{X}(x)=1\}

where
w_{X}
is the sum of the widths.
We also take
\partial^{-}P(\pi)
to be the product
X_{1}(\pi)\times Y(\pi)
.
Let
\partial^{-}P^{(1)}(\pi)
be the area-one locus in
X_{1}(\pi)\times Y(\pi)
.
(We give a more detailed discussion in
Section

D.9
.)

Notation 6.5
.

We take
X_{1}=\bigsqcup_{\pi}X_{1}(\pi)
and
\partial^{-}P^{(1)}=\bigsqcup_{\pi}\partial^{-}P^{(1)}(\pi)
.
∎

Let
\xi
be a Rauzy–Veech sequence starting at
\pi
and ending at
\pi^{\prime}
.
We define
\partial^{-}P^{(1)}(\xi)
to be the subset of
\partial^{-}P^{(1)}(\pi)
whose Rauzy–Veech expansion begins with
\xi
.
Take
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}^{(1)}(\xi)=q_{\pi}(\partial^{-}P^{(1)}(\xi))
.
Let
p^{\pi}
be the projection to widths.
We define
X_{1}(\xi)\subseteq X_{1}(\pi)
to be the image of
\partial^{-}P^{(1)}(\xi)
under
p^{\pi}
.
By composing the Rauzy–Veech renormalisation maps
\RV^{\partial}
(defined in
Section

D.9
) for moves in
\xi
, we obtain a map
\RV^{\partial}_{\xi}\colon X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})\times Y(\xi)\to X_{1}(\xi)\times Y(\pi)
.
Here
Y(\xi)
is defined as in
Equation

5.11
.

Definition 6.6
.

Suppose that
\zeta
is a finite Rauzy–Veech sequence that ends at
\pi
.
Suppose that
\eta
is finite Rauzy–Veech sequence starting from
\pi
.
We define the
\mathcal{S}_{P}(\zeta|\eta)=X_{1}(\eta)\times Y(\zeta)
.
We take
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\zeta|\eta)
to be the area-one locus inside of
\mathcal{S}_{P}(\zeta|\eta)
.
Note that
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\zeta|\eta)
is a subset of
\partial^{-}P^{(1)}(\pi)
.

We finally define
\mathcal{S}(\zeta|\eta)
and
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\zeta|\eta)
to be the images under
q_{\pi}
.
∎

In a slight abuse of notation we use
\pi
to denote the Rauzy–Veech sequence of length zero starting (and ending) at
\pi
.
So, for example,
\mathcal{S}_{P}(\zeta|\pi)=X_{1}(\pi)\times Y(\zeta)
.

Note that
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\zeta|\eta)
is the subset of differentials in
\partial^{-}\mathcal{C}^{(1)}(\pi)
whose backwards and forwards Rauzy–Veech sequences begin with
\zeta
and
\eta
respectively.

We now fix a permutation
\pi
.
By
Corollary

5.15
, we may also fix a directed loop
\theta
, based at
\pi
, which has a positive Rauzy matrix
E_{\theta}
and is strongly nested for
P(\pi)
(see
5.13
).
By extending
\theta
even further (if necessary) we may also assume that
\theta
is
neat
in the sense of
[
AGY06
, Section 4.1.3]
:
that is, if
\theta=\zeta\eta
and
\theta=\eta^{\prime}\zeta
then
\zeta=\theta
.
It follows that, in any Rauzy–Veech sequence, the occurrences of
\theta
do not overlap.

So, suppose that
\theta
is strongly nested, positive, and neat.
We use
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\theta|\theta)
as our Poincaré section.

Suppose that
s
is a reindexing of
\mathcal{A}
.
By
5.7
, the permutation
s
defines an element of the deck group of the cover
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}\to\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
Let
\theta_{s}
be the translate of
\theta
by
s
.
Since the cover is regular (
Lemma

4.64
)
\theta_{s}
is again a loop in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
based at
\pi_{s}=s\circ\pi
.
Thus
\theta_{s}
is again strongly nested, positive, and neat.

Note that all
\theta_{s}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{lab}}
project to
[\theta]
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
It follows that

q_{\pi_{s}}(\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s}))=\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\theta|\theta)

It is often much more convenient to work in the parameter spaces rather than in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
We use
\nu_{P}^{(1)}
to denote the Rauzy–Veech invariant measure on
\partial^{-}P^{(1)}=\bigsqcup_{\pi}\partial^{-}P^{(1)}_{\pi}
defined in
Appendix

D
(right after
D.12
).
Suppose that
s
is a reindexing.
Then
s
induces a map
s\colon X(\pi)\times Y(\pi)\to X(\pi_{s})\times Y(\pi_{s})
preserving all disintegrations discussed in
Appendix

D
.
It follows that
\nu_{P}^{(1)}
is invariant under these maps.
That is, for any measurable set
U\subseteq\partial^{-}P^{(1)}_{\pi}
we have

(6.7)

\nu_{P}^{(1)}(s(U))=\nu_{P}^{(1)}(U)

We now work with the section (in parameters) given by
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
.
By
Equation

6.7
we have

\nu_{P}^{(1)}(\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s}))=\nu_{P}^{(1)}(\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\theta))

Applying this and
D.20
, we have

\nu^{(1)}(\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\theta|\theta))=\nu_{P}^{(1)}(\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\theta))

Lemma 6.8
.

\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\theta)
has finite
\nu_{P}^{(1)}
–volume.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\theta)
lies in
\partial^{-}P^{(1)}(\pi)
it suffices to prove that its
\nu_{\pi}^{(1)}
–volume is finite.
By
Section

D.14
, the measure
\nu^{(1)}_{\pi}
pushes forward under
p^{\pi}
to the measure
\phi_{X_{1}(\pi)}
on
X_{1}(\pi)
.
By
Lemma

D.10
, the measure
\nu_{X_{1}(\pi)}
on
X_{1}(\pi)
is finite.
Also, by
Equation

D.18
we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}\phi_{X_{1}(\pi)}}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{X_{1}(\pi)}}=\vol_{\pi}.

for a smooth function
\vol_{\pi}\colon X_{1}(\pi)\to\mathbb{R}
.

By strong nesting, the closure
\overline{X_{1}(\theta)}
is compact and lies in the interior of
X_{1}(\pi)
.
Hence,
\vol_{\pi}
restricted to
X_{1}(\theta)
is bounded.
Since
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\theta)
is a subset of
\partial^{-}P^{(1)}(\theta)
, it follows that

\int_{\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\theta)}\mathrm{d}\nu_{\pi}^{(1)}<\int_{\partial^{-}P^{(1)}(\theta)}\mathrm{d}\nu_{\pi}^{(1)}=\int_{X_{1}(\theta)}\vol_{\pi}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{X_{1}(\pi)}(x)<\infty

Thus
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\theta)
has finite
\nu_{\pi}^{(1)}
–mass.
∎

6.9.
The pieces of the first return map

As usual (in order to have non-negative matrices) we work with the inverse of the first return map.
On
\partial^{-}P
the first return, by the diagonal flow, is given by
the Rauzy–Veech renormalisation maps
\RV^{\partial}
.
As noted in
Theorem

D.24
the measure
\nu^{(1)}_{P}
is ergodic for
\RV^{\partial}
(restricted to
\partial^{-}P^{(1)}
).
So for a
\nu^{(1)}_{P}
–typical point of
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
the first return is a finite power of
\RV^{\partial}
.
The power required and the polytopes visited depend in piecewise continuous fashion on the given point.
Now we analyse the pieces.

Let
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\theta}
be the set of Rauzy–Veech sequences which

•

start with
\theta_{r}
for some reindexing
r
,

•

end with
\theta_{t}
for some reindexing
t
, and

•

do not contain
\theta_{s}^{2}
for any reindexing
s
.

In particular,
\theta_{s}
(for any reindexing
s
) itself lies in
\Lambda
.

Lemma 6.10
.

Suppose that
\xi
is contained in
\Lambda
.
Suppose that
\xi
is the concatenation
\xi^{\prime}\xi^{\prime\prime}
of non-empty Rauzy–Veech sequences.
Then
\mathcal{S}_{P}(\xi^{\prime}|\xi^{\prime\prime})
is disjoint from
\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
for all reindexings
s
.

Proof.

For any reindexing
s
the sequence
\theta_{s}
is neat.
Note that
\xi
does not contain
\theta_{s}^{2}
.
The lemma follows.
∎

The next result follows from the definitions. See
Figure

6.12
.

Lemma 6.11
.

Suppose that
\xi
is contained in
\Lambda
.
Suppose that
r
and
t
are reindexings so that
\xi
starts with
\theta_{r}
and ends with
\theta_{t}
.
Then we have the following:

•

\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{r}\xi|\theta_{t})
is a subset of
\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{t}|\theta_{t})
,

•

\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{r}|\xi\theta_{t})
is a subset of
\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{r}|\theta_{r})
and

•

\RV^{\partial}_{\xi}(\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{r}\xi|\theta_{t}))=\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{r}|\xi\theta_{t})
.∎

Figure 6.12
.
The domain and range of the piece
\RV^{\partial}_{\xi}
.

By
Lemmas

6.10
and
6.11
, we deduce that
\RV^{\partial}_{\xi}\colon\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{r}\xi|\theta_{t})\to\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{r}|\xi\theta_{t})
is the inverse of a piece of the first return map to
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
. This also holds for the restriction to the area-one locus: namely,
\RV^{\partial}_{\xi}\colon\mathcal{S}_{P}^{(1)}(\theta_{r}\xi|\theta_{t})\to\mathcal{S}_{P}^{(1)}(\theta_{r}|\xi\theta_{t})
.

Lemma 6.13
.

Suppose that
(x,y)
is a parameter in
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
.
Suppose that there is a
T>0
so that
g_{T}(x,y)
again lies in
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
.
Then there is a least such
T
.
Furthermore, the flow segment
\gamma
from
(x,y)
to
g_{T}(x,y)
is a
\xi
–segment for some
\xi
in the alphabet
\Lambda
.

Proof.

By
Lemmas

4.67
and
4.74
, the least positive
T
such that
g_{T}(x,y)
lies in
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
exists.
Since
\gamma
returns to
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
, we have the following.

•

By
4.78
the Rauzy–Veech sequence
\xi=\xi(\gamma)
is well defined.

•

By definition of the section,
\xi
starts with some
\theta_{r}
and ends with some
\theta_{t}
.

Suppose that
\xi
contains
\theta_{s}^{2}
.
We write
\xi
as a finite concatenation
\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\dotsb\xi_{r}
where each
\xi_{i}
is in
\Lambda
.
It follows that there exists a positive time
S<T
such that

•

the segment from
(x,y)
to
g_{S}(x,y)
is a
\xi_{1}
–segment, and

•

g_{S}(x,y)
lies in
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
.

This contradicts the fact that
g_{T}(x,y)
is the first return.
It follows that
\xi
is in
\Lambda
, as desired.
∎

With
Lemma

6.13
in hand, the following is well-defined.

Definition 6.14
.

Suppose that
\xi
is in
\Lambda
.
Suppose that
(x,y)
lies in
\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{r}|\xi\theta_{t})
.
Suppose that
\RV^{\partial}_{\xi}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=(x,y)
, where
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
lies in
\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{r}\xi|\theta_{t})
.
Then the first return of
g_{T}(x,y)
to
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
occurs at time

\rho(x,y)=\log(w(E_{\xi}x^{\prime}))

Note that this is a
\nu_{P}
–measurable function on
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
.
∎

We now obtain the following.

Lemma 6.15
.

Suppose that
(x,y)
is a point in
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
which has a first return to
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
.
Then
\rho(x,y)>\log 2
.

Proof.

Let
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
be the first return of
g_{t}(x,y)
to the section.
By
Lemma

6.13
, the flow segment from
(x,y)
to
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
is a
\xi
–segment for some
\xi
in
\Pi
.
Suppose that
E_{\xi}
is its Rauzy matrix.
Thus
x=(E_{\xi}x^{\prime})/w(E_{\xi}x^{\prime})
and the return time is
\rho(x,y)=\log w(E_{\xi}x^{\prime})
.
(Here we suppress the heights to simplify the notation.)
Since
\xi
is in
\Pi
, it can be written as a concatenation
\xi^{\prime}\theta
, where
\xi^{\prime}
could be empty.
Thus
E_{\xi}
factors as
E_{\xi^{\prime}}E_{\theta}
.
Note that
E_{\xi^{\prime}}
is an integral, non-negative, invertible matrix.
We now compute as follows.

\log w(E_{\xi}x^{\prime})=\log w(E_{\xi^{\prime}}E_{\theta}x^{\prime})\geqslant\log w(E_{\theta}x^{\prime})>\log|\mathcal{A}|\geqslant\log 2\qed

By ergodicity of the map
\RV^{\partial}
(see
Theorem

D.24
), a
\nu^{(1)}_{P}
–typical point of
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
returns to
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
infinitely often (forwards and backwards).
Hence, the disjoint union
\bigsqcup_{\xi\in\Lambda}\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{r}|\xi\theta_{t})
has full measure in
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
.

The measure
\nu^{(1)}_{P}
is invariant under the Rauzy–Veech renormalisation (see
D.13
).
Since each first return to
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
is given by a finite Rauzy–Veech sequence, we conclude that the restriction of
\nu^{(1)}_{P}
to
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
is invariant under first returns.

6.16.
Distortion

In order to control the measure distortion of the pieces
\RV^{\partial}_{\xi}
(for
\xi
in
\Lambda
) we first consider the measure distortion of the Rauzy maps
\R^{\partial}_{\xi}
.

We use the notation of
Section

D.14
.
Let
\zeta
be a finite Rauzy sequence that starts at
\pi
and ends at
\pi^{\prime}
.
By composing the renormalisation maps for each move in
\zeta
, we obtain the Rauzy renormalisation map
\R^{\partial}_{\zeta}\colon X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})\to X_{1}(\zeta)
.
Again, we take
E_{\zeta}
to be the associated Rauzy matrix.
Recall that
w_{X}\colon X_{1}(\pi)\to\mathbb{R}
is the sum of the widths.
We deduce the following:

\R^{\partial}_{\zeta}(x^{\prime})=\frac{E_{\zeta}x^{\prime}}{w_{X}(E_{\zeta}x^{\prime})}

We use
\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})
to denote the Jacobian of
\R^{\partial}_{\zeta}
; that is,

\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})(x^{\prime})=\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{X_{1}(\pi)}}{\mathrm{d}[(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})_{*}\nu_{X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})}]}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta}(x^{\prime}))

Definition 6.17
.

Let
K\geqslant 1
.
We say that a Rauzy–Veech sequence
\zeta
from
\pi
to
\pi^{\prime}
has
K
–bounded distortion
if

\frac{\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})(x)}{\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})(x^{\prime})}\leqslant K

for any pair of points
x,x^{\prime}\in X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})
.
∎

The next lemma says that probabilities, conditioned on a sequence having
K
–bounded distortion, are preserved (up to multiplication by
K^{2}
).

Lemma 6.18
.

Suppose that
\zeta
is Rauzy–Veech sequence from
\pi
to
\pi^{\prime}
.
Suppose that
\zeta
has
K
–bounded distortion.
Suppose that
U^{\prime}
is any measurable subset of
X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})
.
Then, taking
\nu=\nu_{X_{1}(\pi)}
and
\nu^{\prime}=\nu_{X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})}
, we have

\frac{1}{K^{2}}\cdot\frac{\nu^{\prime}(U^{\prime})}{\nu^{\prime}(X_{1}(\pi^{\prime}))}\leqslant\frac{\nu(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta}(U^{\prime}))}{\nu(X_{1}(\zeta))}\leqslant K^{2}\cdot\frac{\nu^{\prime}(U^{\prime})}{\nu^{\prime}(X_{1}(\pi^{\prime}))}

Proof.

We fix a point
x_{0}
in
X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})
.
Since
\zeta
has
K
–bounded distortion, it follows that for any
x\in X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})
,

\frac{1}{K}\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})(x_{0})\leqslant\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})(x)\leqslant K\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})(x_{0})

Suppose that
V^{\prime}
is any measurable subset of
X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})
with positive measure.
From the definitions and by the change of coordinates formula we have the following:

\displaystyle\nu(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta}(V^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\int_{\R^{\partial}_{\zeta}(V^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}\nu

\displaystyle=\int_{\R^{\partial}_{\zeta}(V^{\prime})}\mathcal{J}(R^{\partial}_{\zeta})\mathrm{d}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})_{*}\nu

\displaystyle=\int_{V^{\prime}}\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})\mathrm{d}\nu^{\prime}

By
Lemma

D.10
, all the integrals above are finite.
This and the preceding bounds on the Jacobian give the following:

\frac{1}{K}\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})(x_{0})\leqslant\frac{\nu(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta}(V^{\prime}))}{\nu^{\prime}(V^{\prime})}\leqslant K\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})(x_{0})

We now suppose (as we may) that
U^{\prime}
has positive measure.
We apply the above estimate twice:
once with
V^{\prime}=U^{\prime}
and once with
V^{\prime}=X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})
(noting that
\RV^{\partial}_{\zeta}(X_{1}(\pi^{\prime}))=X_{1}(\zeta)
) to get

\frac{1}{K}\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})(x_{0})\leqslant\frac{\nu(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta}(U^{\prime}))}{\nu^{\prime}(U^{\prime})}\leqslant K\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})(x_{0})

and

\frac{1}{K}\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})(x_{0})\leqslant\frac{\nu(X_{1}(\zeta))}{\nu^{\prime}(X_{1}(\pi^{\prime}))}\leqslant K\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})(x_{0})

The conclusion follows.
∎

Corollary 6.19
.

Suppose that
\zeta
is a Rauzy–Veech sequence from
\pi
to
\pi^{\prime}
and
\zeta^{\prime}
a finite Rauzy–Veech sequence that starts from
\pi^{\prime}
.
Suppose that
\zeta
has
K
–bounded distortion. Then, taking
\nu=\nu_{X_{1}(\pi)}
and
\nu^{\prime}=\nu_{X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})}
,
we get

\frac{1}{K^{2}}\cdot\frac{\nu^{\prime}(X_{1}(\zeta^{\prime}))}{\nu^{\prime}(X_{1}(\pi^{\prime}))}\leqslant\frac{\nu(X_{1}(\zeta\zeta^{\prime}))}{\nu(X_{1}(\zeta))}\leqslant K^{2}\cdot\frac{\nu^{\prime}(X_{1}(\zeta^{\prime}))}{\nu^{\prime}(X_{1}(\pi^{\prime}))}

Proof.

Take
U^{\prime}=X_{1}(\zeta^{\prime})
and thus find
\R^{\partial}_{\zeta}(U^{\prime})=X_{1}(\zeta\zeta^{\prime})
.
The corollary follows directly by
Lemma

6.18
.
∎

Suppose that
\zeta
is a Rauzy–Veech sequence from
\pi
to
\pi^{\prime}
with Rauzy matrix
E_{\zeta}
.
In the abelian case, by
[
Vee78
, Proposition 5.2]
, we have for
x\in X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})
that

(6.20)

\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})(x)=\frac{1}{w_{X}(E_{\zeta}x)^{|\mathcal{A}|}}

In the quadratic case, by
[
Gad12
, Equation 8.3 and Lemma 8.1]
, there exists a constant
c_{\zeta}>0
such that

(6.21)

\mathcal{J}(\R^{\partial}_{\zeta})(x)=\frac{c_{\zeta}}{w_{X}(E_{\zeta}x)^{|\mathcal{A}|-1}}

The appearance of the constant
c_{\zeta}
arises from the width equality in (
4.32
).

Suppose that
\zeta
is a Rauzy–Veech sequence from
\pi
to
\pi^{\prime}
.
Suppose that
\alpha
is a label in
\mathcal{A}
.
We define
\col_{\alpha}(E_{\zeta})
to be the
\alpha
–column of
E_{\zeta}
.
Suppose that
k\geqslant 1
is a constant.
The matrix
E_{\zeta}
is said to be
k
–balanced
if

\frac{\|\col_{\alpha}(E_{\zeta})\|_{1}}{\|\col_{\alpha^{\prime}}(E_{\zeta})\|_{1}}\leqslant k

for any
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 6.22
.

Suppose that
\zeta
is a Rauzy–Veech sequence from
\pi
to
\pi^{\prime}
.
Suppose that the Rauzy matrix
E_{\zeta}
is
k
–balanced.
Then
\zeta
has
K
–bounded distortion for
K=k^{|\mathcal{A}|}
in the abelian case and for
K=k^{|\mathcal{A}|-1}
in the quadratic case.

Proof.

Since
E_{\zeta}
is non-negative, for every
x\in X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})
we have the following

w_{X}(E_{\zeta}x)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}x_{\alpha}\|\col_{\alpha}(E_{\zeta})\|_{1}

Since
w_{X}(x)=1
for all
x\in X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})
, it follows that

\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\|\col_{\alpha}(E_{\zeta})\|_{1}\leqslant w_{X}(E_{\zeta}x)\leqslant\max_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\|\col_{\alpha}(E_{\zeta})\|_{1}

Therefore,

\frac{w_{X}(E_{\zeta}x)}{w_{X}(E_{\zeta}x^{\prime})}\leqslant k

for every
x,x^{\prime}\in X_{1}(\pi^{\prime})
.

In either case (abelian or quadratic), the lemma follows from
Equation

6.20
or
Equation

6.21
, with
K=k^{|\mathcal{A}|}
or
K=k^{|\mathcal{A}|-1}
, respectively.
∎

We now use our (strongly nested, positive, neat) sequence
\theta
.

Lemma 6.23
.

Suppose that
\zeta\theta_{s}
(for some reindexing
s
) is a finite Rauzy–Veech sequence. Then there is
k_{\theta}\geqslant 1
, depending only on
\theta
, such that
E_{\zeta\theta_{s}}
is
k_{\theta}
–balanced.

Proof.

Suppose that
M
is a non-negative matrix with rows and columns indexed by
\mathcal{A}
.
We define
\|M\|_{1}=\max_{\alpha}\|\col_{\alpha}(M)\|_{1}
.
We denote the
(\alpha,\beta)
entry of
M
by
M(\alpha,\beta)
.
We define
M_{\min}=\min_{\alpha,\beta}M(\alpha,\beta)
.

Note that for any reindexing
s
, we have
\|E_{\theta_{s}}\|_{1}=\|E_{\theta}\|_{1}
and
(E_{\theta_{s}})_{\min}=(E_{\theta})_{\min}
.
Since
E_{\theta}
is a positive integer matrix, we have
(E_{\theta})_{\min}\geqslant 1
.

Now note that
E_{\zeta\theta_{s}}=E_{\zeta}E_{\theta_{s}}
, so

\col_{\beta}(E_{\zeta\theta_{s}})=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}E_{\theta_{s}}(\alpha,\beta)\col_{\alpha}(E_{\zeta})

We deduce that, for any
\beta\in\mathcal{A}
,

\|E_{\zeta}\|_{1}\leqslant(E_{\theta_{s}})_{\min}\cdot\|E_{\zeta}\|_{1}\leqslant\|\col_{\beta}(E_{\zeta\theta_{s}})\|_{1}\leqslant\|E_{\theta_{s}}\|_{1}\cdot\|E_{\zeta}\|_{1}

It follows that
E_{\zeta\theta_{s}}
is
k_{\theta}
–balanced for
k_{\theta}=\|E_{\theta}\|_{1}
.
∎

From this we deduce the following.

Corollary 6.24
.

Suppose that
u=\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{r}
is a finite Rauzy–Veech sequence obtained by concatenating
\xi_{j}
, all in
\Lambda
.
Then there is
k_{\theta}\geqslant 1
, depending only on
\theta
, such that
E_{u}
is
k_{\theta}
–balanced.

Proof.

Note that
\xi_{r}
ends with
\theta_{t}
for some reindexing
t
.
So we may write
u
as
\zeta\theta_{t}
, for a (possibly empty) Rauzy sequence
\zeta
.
The corollary follows from
Lemma

6.23
.
∎

From this and
Lemma

6.22
we have the following.

Corollary 6.25
.

Suppose that
u=\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{r}
where all
\xi_{j}
are in
\Lambda
.
Then, with
K
as in
Lemma

6.22
, the sequence
u
has
K
–bounded distortion. ∎

We now consider our section
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
.
Recall that
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\pi_{s})
is the area-one locus in
X_{1}(\pi_{s})\times Y(\theta_{s})
.
Let
Y(\theta_{s},x)
denote the preimage of
x\in X_{1}(\pi_{s})
under the (restriction of) projection to the first factor.
Note that
Y(\theta_{s},x)
is a subset of
Y(\pi_{s},x)
,
as defined in
Section

D.14
.
By
Lemma

D.16
, we have

\nu_{Y(\pi_{s},x)}(Y(\theta_{s},x))<\nu_{Y(\pi_{s},x)}(Y(\pi_{s},x))<\infty

We define the function
\vol_{\theta_{s}}\colon X_{1}(\pi_{s})\to\mathbb{R}
by
\vol_{\theta_{s}}(x)=\nu_{Y(\pi_{s},x)}(Y(\theta_{s},x))
.
Note that
\vol_{\theta_{s}}
is smooth.

Lemma 6.26
.

There exist constants
L_{\theta},U_{\theta}>0
depending only on
\theta

so that, for any reindexing
s
and any
x\in X_{1}(\theta_{s})
, we have

L_{\theta}\leqslant\vol_{\theta_{s}}(x)\leqslant U_{\theta}

Proof.

Observe that, by
Equation

6.7
, we have
\vol_{\theta_{s}}(s(x))=\vol_{\theta}(x)
for every
x\in X_{1}(\pi)
.
Note that
\vol_{\theta}(x)>0
for any
x\in X_{1}(\pi)
.
The lemma follows from the fact that
\overline{X_{1}(\theta)}
is compact and contained in the interior of
X_{1}(\pi)
.
∎

We now consider the area-one locus in
X_{1}(\pi_{s})\times Y(\pi_{s})
with the measure
\nu^{(1)}_{P}
.
As in
Section

D.14
(immediately before
Equation

D.18
), the measure pushes forward (under projection to widths) to the measure
\phi_{X_{1}(\pi_{s})}
.
By
Equation

D.18
, the measure
\phi_{X_{1}(\pi_{s})}
has density
\vol_{\pi_{s}}(x)
with respect to
\nu_{X_{1}(\pi_{s})}
.
It follows that when restricted to
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\pi_{s})
the measure
\nu^{(1)}_{P}
pushes forward to the measure
\vol_{\theta_{s}}(x)\cdot\nu_{X_{1}(\pi_{s})}
.

Lemma 6.27
.

Suppose that
\zeta
is a finite Rauzy–Veech sequence that starts at
\pi_{s}
.
Then we have:

L_{\theta}\cdot\nu_{X_{1}(\pi_{s})}(X_{1}(\zeta))\leqslant\nu^{(1)}_{P}(\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\zeta))\leqslant U_{\theta}\cdot\nu_{X_{1}(\pi_{s})}(X_{1}(\zeta))

Proof.

Note that
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\zeta)
is a subset of
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\pi_{s})
.
By definition of the pushforward measure we have the following:

\int_{\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\zeta)}\mathrm{d}\nu^{(1)}_{P}=\int_{X_{1}(\zeta)}\vol_{\theta_{s}}\mathrm{d}\nu_{X_{1}(\pi_{s})}

The conclusion now follows from the bounds on
\vol_{\theta_{s}}
in
Lemma

6.26
.
∎

6.28.
The alphabet for the countable shift

Recall the set
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\theta}
defined at the beginning of
Section

6.9
.
Reindexing defines an equivalence relation on
\Lambda
.
We denote the set of equivalence classes as
\Pi
.
Each equivalence class contains a unique representative that begins with
\theta
.
Hence, we may identify
\Pi
with the set of Rauzy–Veech sequences which

•

start with
\theta
,

•

end with
\theta_{t}
for some reindexing
t
, and

•

do not contain
\theta^{2}_{s}
for any reindexing
s
.

Suppose that
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
are elements of
\Pi
.
Suppose that
\xi
ends with
\theta_{t}
.
Let
\xi_{t}^{\prime}
be the reindexing of
\xi^{\prime}
by
t
.
Then we define the concatenation
\xi\xi^{\prime}\in\Pi^{2}
to be the Rauzy-Veech sequence
\xi\xi^{\prime}_{t}
.
We now recursively extend this to obtain finite words over
\Pi
.

As a piece of simplifying notation we define

\nu^{(1)}_{P}(\eta|\zeta)=\nu^{(1)}_{P}(\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\eta|\zeta))

Lemma 6.29
.

There exists a constant
M\geqslant 1
, depending only on
\theta
, with the following property.
Suppose that
u
and
v
are words in
\Pi^{i}
and
\Pi^{j}
respectively.
Then we have the following:

\frac{1}{M}\leqslant\frac{\nu_{P}^{(1)}(\theta|uv)}{\nu^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|u)\cdot\nu_{P}^{(1)}(\theta|v)}\leqslant M

Proof.

We take
\nu=\nu_{X_{1}(\pi)}
.
Suppose that
u
ends with
\theta_{t}
.
The concatenation
uv
defines the Rauzy–Veech sequence
uv_{t}
.
By
Lemma

6.27
applied to
uv_{t}
we have:

L_{\theta}\cdot\nu(X_{1}(uv_{t}))\leqslant\nu_{P}^{(1)}(\theta|uv_{t})\leqslant U_{\theta}\cdot\nu(X_{1}(uv_{t}))

We take
\nu_{t}=\nu_{X_{1}(\pi_{t})}
.
By
Corollaries

6.25
and
6.19
applied to the concatenated sequence
uv_{t}
we get

\frac{1}{m}\cdot\nu(X_{1}(u))\cdot\nu_{t}(X_{1}(v))\leqslant\nu(X_{1}(uv_{t}))\leqslant m\cdot\nu(X_{1}(u))\cdot\nu_{t}(X_{1}(v))

By
Lemma

6.27
, we have

\frac{1}{U_{\theta}}\cdot\nu^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|u)\leqslant\nu(X_{1}(u))

Similarly for
v_{t}
, we have

\frac{1}{U_{\theta}}\cdot\nu^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{t}|v_{t})\leqslant\nu_{t}(X_{1}(v_{t}))

Similarly, we have

\nu(X_{1}(u))\leqslant\frac{1}{L_{\theta}}\cdot\nu^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|u)

and

\nu_{t}(X_{1}(v_{t}))\leqslant\frac{1}{L_{\theta}}\cdot\nu^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{t}|v_{t})

Combining the above estimates, we get

\frac{L_{\theta}}{mU_{\theta}^{2}}\cdot\nu^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|u)\cdot\nu^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|v)\leqslant\nu^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|uv_{t})\leqslant\frac{mU_{\theta}}{L_{\theta}^{2}}\cdot\nu^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|u)\cdot\nu^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|v)

which concludes the proof by taking
M=\max\left\{\frac{mU_{\theta}^{2}}{L_{\theta}},\frac{mU_{\theta}}{L_{\theta}^{2}}\right\}\geqslant 1
.
∎

6.30.
Contraction

The discussion above, restricted to abelian components, reproduces the work of
[
AGY06
, Sections 4.1 and 4.2]
.
In this section and the next we give a (partial) dictionary between our language and theirs.

Recall (from
Section

D.2
, immediately after
D.3
) that the width and height cones do not contain a linear subspace.
It follows that their Hilbert metric is well-defined.
The pieces of the first return to
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
depend only on the widths.
So it suffices to consider the Hilbert metric on
X_{1}(\pi_{s})
for each
\pi_{s}
.
For definitions and background, see
[
AGY06
, Section 4.2.1]
.

By positivity of
E_{\theta_{s}}
, there is a positive constant
c_{\theta}<1
(that depends only on
\theta
) such that in the Hilbert metric on
X_{1}(\pi_{s})
we have that

•

the cone
X_{1}(\theta_{s})
has diameter bounded above by
c_{\theta}
and

•

for any
u
in
\Pi^{r}
(that ends in say
\theta_{t}
), the cone
X_{1}(u\theta_{t})
has diameter bounded above by
c_{\theta}^{r+1}
.

We call the diameter bounds the
uniform contraction property
.

6.31.
Summary of the coding

We summarise our coding for the diagonal flow.
Again, for notational convenience, we work in parameter spaces.

•

Our section
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
admits a (full measure) countable partition.
The pieces of this partition are the sets
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{r}|\xi\theta_{t})
where
\xi
in
\Lambda
starts with
\theta_{r}
and ends with
\theta_{t}
.

•

The inverse of the map
\RV^{\partial}_{\xi}
restricted to
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{r}|\xi\theta_{t})
gives a piece of the first return to
\bigsqcup_{s}\,\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
(with image
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{r}\xi|\theta_{t})
).

•

By the uniform contraction property, the pieces of first return give a
uniformly expanding Markov map
(in the sense of
[
AGY06
, Definition 2.2]
).

•

The maps
\RV^{\partial}_{\xi}
(where
\xi
ranges over
\Lambda
) give a
hyperbolic skew-product
(in the sense of
[
AGY06
, Definition 2.5]
) over the expanding Markov map.

Taking the image under
q_{P}=\bigsqcup_{\pi}q_{\pi}
, we get the first return map for our section in differentials.
We denote this by
F_{\theta}\colon\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\theta|\theta)\to\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\theta|\theta)
.
By
Lemma

6.10
,
Lemma

6.11
and
Lemma

6.13
, the pieces of
F_{\theta}
are in bijection with
\Pi
.
(Note that this is only defined up to sets with
\nu^{(1)}
measure zero).
Recall that
\Sigma=\Pi^{\mathbb{Z}}
and
S
is the shift on
\Sigma
.
Through the partition of
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\theta|\theta)
, we obtain a conjugacy from
(\Sigma,\text{{S}})
to
(\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\theta|\theta),F_{\theta})
.

Remark 6.32
.

We pushforward (by the inverse of the bijection) the measure
\nu^{(1)}
on
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}(\theta|\theta)
to obtain a measure
\mu
on
\Sigma
.
By
D.20
and
Lemma

6.29
, we deduce that
\mu
has bounded distortion.
∎

6.33.
The first return time function

We now restrict to
\mathcal{S}_{P}^{(1)}(\theta|\theta)
(instead of
\bigsqcup_{s}\mathcal{S}_{P}^{(1)}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
.
We now define a simpler alphabet.
Let
\Pi^{\prime}
be the set of finite Rauzy–Veech sequences which

•

start with
\theta
,

•

end with
\theta
, and

•

do not contain
\theta^{2}
.

Following
Lemma

6.10
,
Lemma

6.11
, and
Lemma

6.13
, the pieces of the first return to
\mathcal{S}_{P}^{(1)}(\theta|\theta)
are given by the alphabet
\Pi^{\prime}
.
By
Corollary

6.25
, any sequence
\eta
that lies in
\Pi^{\prime}
has
K
–bounded distortion where
K
depends only on
\theta
.
Suppose that
(x,y)
lies in
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\eta\theta)
.
Suppose that
\RV^{\partial}_{\eta}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=(x,y)
.
The return time for
(x,y)
, under diagonal flow, is given by

(6.34)

\rho^{\prime}(x,y)=\log(w(E_{\eta}x^{\prime}))

Suppose that
\xi
lies in
\Pi
. Suppose that
\xi
ends with
\theta_{t}
for some reindexing
t
.
We define
\Pi^{\prime}_{\xi}
to be those
\eta
in
\Pi^{\prime}
that start with
\xi
.
The union
\bigsqcup_{\eta\in\Pi^{\prime}_{\xi}}\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\eta\theta)
has full
\nu^{(1)}_{P}
–measure in
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\xi\theta_{t})
.

Recall (from
6.14
) that we use
\rho
to denote the first return time to
\bigsqcup_{s}\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta_{s}|\theta_{s})
.
Suppose that
(x,y)
lies in some
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\eta\theta)
for some
\eta
in
\Pi^{\prime}_{\xi}
.
It follows that
\rho(x,y)\leqslant\rho^{\prime}(x,y)
.
We deduce that

\int_{\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\xi\theta)}\rho(x,y)\,d\nu^{(1)}_{P}\leqslant\sum_{\eta\in\Pi^{\prime}_{\xi}}\int_{\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\eta\theta)}\rho^{\prime}(x,y)\,d\nu^{(1)}_{P}

So, to prove
\nu^{(1)}_{P}
–integrability of
\rho
, it suffices to prove
\nu^{(1)}_{P}
–integrability of
\rho^{\prime}
.
We do this in
Section

6.35
and
Section

6.37
.

6.35.
The key measure estimate

We state the key estimate needed to show
\nu^{(1)}_{P}
–integrability of
\rho^{\prime}
.
From this we will deduce the
\log
–integrability of the discrete cocycles in
Section

7.13
.

We organise
\eta
in
\Pi^{\prime}
by the size of
\|E_{\eta}\|_{1}
, on a logarithmic scale, as follows.
Given
S>1
and
k\geqslant 1
, we denote

\Pi^{\prime}_{k}(S)=\{\eta\in\Pi^{\prime}\mid S^{k-1}\leqslant\|E_{\eta}\|_{1}<S^{k}\}

Applying the recurrence estimates of
[
Gad12
, Propositions 10.21 and 10.33]
, there exist constants
S>1
,
C>0
, and
0<c<1
that depend only on
\theta
such that, for any
k\geqslant 1
, we have the following:

\sum_{\eta\in\Pi^{\prime}_{k}(S)}\nu_{X_{1}(\pi)}(X_{1}(\eta))<C\cdot c^{k}

By
Lemma

6.26
, the density
\vol_{\theta}
is bounded above and below on
X_{1}(\theta)
.
So, by
Lemma

6.27
,
a similar estimate holds for
\nu^{(1)}_{P}
; namely

(6.36)

\sum_{\eta\in\Pi^{\prime}_{k}(S)}\nu_{P}^{(1)}(\theta|\eta\theta)<U_{\theta}C\cdot c^{k}

6.37.
The first-return time is integrable

Definition 6.38
.

Suppose that
(\Sigma,\mu)
is a measure space.
Suppose that
\rho\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
is non-negative and
\mu
–measurable.
We say that
\rho
has
exponential tails
if there exists
h>0
such that

\int_{\Sigma}e^{h\rho}\,\mathrm{d}\mu<\infty\qed

Lemma 6.39
.

The first return time
\rho^{\prime}
has exponential tails over
(\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\theta),\nu^{(1)}_{P})
.
In particular,
\rho^{\prime}
is integrable over
(\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\theta),\nu^{(1)}_{P})
.

Proof.

Let
U_{\theta}
,
S
,
C
,
c
be the constants in
Equation

6.36
.
Suppose that
\eta
is in
\Pi^{\prime}_{k}(S)
.
Suppose that
(x,y)
lies in
\mathcal{S}_{P}^{(1)}(\theta|\eta\theta)
.
Let
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{P}^{(1)}(\theta\eta|\theta)
be the first-return of
(x,y)
.
Then

\rho^{\prime}(x,y)=\log(w(E_{\eta}x^{\prime}))\leqslant\log\|E_{\eta}\|_{1}<\log S^{k}=k\log S

Given
h>0
, the recurrence estimate in
Equation

6.36
shows that

\int_{\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\theta)}e^{h\rho^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{(1)}_{P}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\,\sum_{\eta\in\Pi^{\prime}_{k}(S)}\int_{\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{P}(\theta|\eta\theta)}e^{h\rho^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{(1)}_{P}<\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}U_{\theta}C(S^{h}c)^{k}

The series on the right converges exactly when
S^{h}c<1
.
∎

7.
Cocycles

7.1.
Hodge bundles

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is a stratum component.
Fix a differential
q
in
\mathcal{C}
;
let
Z(q)
be the set of singularities.
Fix also a surface
S
, a finite set
Z(S)\subseteq S
, and a homeomorphism
f_{q}\colon(S,Z(S))\to(q,Z(q))
.
Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(S,Z(S))
be the moduli space of Riemann surfaces marked at
|Z(S)|
–many unlabelled points.
Let
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}(S,Z(S))
be the corresponding Teichmüller space:
that is, points of
\mathcal{T}
are (equivalence classes of) Riemann surfaces
(X,Z(X))
equipped with a marking homeomorphism
f_{X}\colon(S,Z(S))\to(X,Z(X))
.
Recall that
\mathcal{T}
is the universal (orbifold) cover of
\mathcal{M}
.
We use
\mathrm{Mod}(S,Z(S))
to denote the deck group (acting on the right).
Thought of as a mapping class group,
\mathrm{Mod}(S,Z(S))
acts transitively on the points of
Z(S)
.

The
(relative) Hodge bundle

{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}
over
\mathcal{T}
is the vector bundle which, at a point
[X,Z(X),f_{X}]\in\mathcal{T}
, has fibre
\mathrm{H}^{1}(X,Z(X);\mathbb{R})
.
The markings give a trivialisation of
{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}
:
namely, at
[X,Z(X),f_{X}]\in\mathcal{T}
, the map
f_{X}\colon(S,Z(S))\to(X,Z(X))
induces an isomorphism

f_{X}^{*}\colon\mathrm{H}^{1}(X,Z(X);\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(S,Z(S);\mathbb{R})

This, in turn, induces an isomorphism of vector bundles
{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}\to\mathcal{T}\times\mathrm{H}^{1}(S,Z(S);\mathbb{R})
.
Note that
\mathrm{Mod}(S,Z(S))
acts on the trivial bundle (again on the right), and thus also on
{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}
.
The quotient
\mathrm{H}
is an (orbifold) vector bundle over
\mathcal{M}
.
We call
\mathrm{H}
the
(relative) Hodge bundle
over
\mathcal{M}
.

Suppose now that
\mathcal{L}
is an orbifold equipped with a map to
\mathcal{M}
.
We pullback
\mathrm{H}
to obtain an orbifold vector bundle
\mathrm{H}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
In a small abuse of notation, we take
\mathrm{H}_{\mathcal{C}}
to be the orbifold vector bundle obtained when
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
We call
\mathrm{H}_{\mathcal{C}}
the
(relative) Hodge bundle
over
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

The orbifold vector bundle
\mathrm{H}_{\mathcal{C}}
is a vector bundle.

Proof.

We trace through the construction.
Let
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}_{\mathrm{root}}
be the cover of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
obtained by equipping differentials
((q,v),Z(q))
with marking homeomorphisms
f_{q}
and then taking equivalence classes.
Thus there is a natural map from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}_{\mathrm{root}}
to
\mathcal{T}
that takes a marked differential
[(q,v),Z(q),f_{q}]
to a marked Riemann surface
[X,Z(X),f_{X}]
.
We form the
(relative) Hodge bundle

{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}_{\mathcal{C}}
over
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}_{\mathrm{root}}
by pulling back
{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}}\to\mathcal{T}
.
Thus the fibre over
[(q,v),Z(q),f_{q}]
is
\mathrm{H}^{1}(X,Z(X);\mathbb{R})
.
As before, we trivialise using the markings, and then quotient by the action of the mapping class group
\mathrm{Mod}(S,Z(S))
.
Finally, the mapping class group acts without fixed points on
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
So
\mathrm{H}_{\mathcal{C}}
is a vector bundle, as desired.
∎

7.3.
Orientation covers

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is a stratum component of quadratic differentials.
Suppose that
q
lies in
\mathcal{C}
.
Then
q
has a canonical
orientation cover
, say
p
.
(The cover
p
is branched over every pole, and over every zero of odd order, of
q
.)
We take
Z(p)
to be the full preimage of
Z(q)
.
(Note that this differs from the discussion in
[
AR12
, Section 7.4.2]
.)
Note that
p
is an abelian differential.
Let
\iota_{p}
be the deck transformation of the cover
p\to q
;
note that
\iota_{p}
is an involution.
We use
\pm\omega_{p}
to denote the two cohomology classes whose periods give
p
.
For further details, see
[
Lan04a
, Construction, page 519]
.

Fix a surface
R
, a finite set
Z(R)\subseteq R
, and a homeomorphism
f_{p}\colon(R,Z(R))\to(p,Z(p))
.
Let
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}(R,Z(R))
be the corresponding Teichmüller space and let
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}(R,Z(R))
.
Let
\iota\colon R\to R
be the pullback of the involution
\iota_{p}
.
Note that the isotopy class of
\iota
is independent of
p
;
thus
\iota
gives a well-defined mapping class
[\iota]
in
\mathrm{Mod}(R,Z(R))
.

We now define
\mathcal{B}
to be the stratum component of abelian differentials that contains
p
.
It is unclear how to realise
\mathcal{C}
as (an isomorphic copy of) a locus in
\mathcal{B}
.
To address this we resort to
bi-rooted
differentials.

We define
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{biroot}}
to be the following stratum of differentials:
a point
(p,u,u^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{biroot}}
is an abelian differential
p
in
\mathcal{B}
equipped with (distinct, unlabelled) roots
u
and
u^{\prime}
.
As usual the roots point along some horizontal separatrix.
Furthermore, we require that
u
points in the positive horizontal direction if and only if
u^{\prime}
points in the negative horizontal direction.

Lemma 7.4
.

The stratum
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{biroot}}
is a manifold.

Proof.

Orbifold points in strata of differentials only arise from translation symmetries.
∎

The map from
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
to
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{biroot}}
, taking
(q,v)
to its orientation cover, is an isomorphism onto its image: see
[
AR12
, Section 7.4.3]
.
We use
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{root}}
to denote the image.

Let
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}
be the (relative) Hodge bundle over
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.
Note that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{root}}
has a forgetful map to
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.
Let
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}
be the pullback of
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}
.
We again call
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}
the
(relative) Hodge bundle
over
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
As in
Lemma

7.2
, we have the following.

Lemma 7.5
.

The bundle
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}
is a vector bundle. ∎

Recall that the fibre of
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}
over
(p,u,u^{\prime})
is
\mathrm{H}^{1}(p,Z(p);\mathbb{R})
which is isomorphic to
\mathrm{H}^{1}(R,Z(R);\mathbb{R})
.
Since
\iota_{p}
is an involution, so is
\iota_{p}^{*}
acting on cohomology.
It thus splits each fibre of
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}
into
+1
and
-1
eigenspaces: we call these the
plus
and
minus fibres
.
Since the maps
\iota_{p}
, for
p\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{root}}
, induce a single isotopy class
[\iota]
in
\mathrm{Mod}(R,Z(R))
, the plus and minus fibres induce a splitting of the vector bundle
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}
.
We define the
plus piece

\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{+}
and the
minus piece

\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{-}
to be the vector bundles induced by the plus and minus fibres respectively.

7.6.
Symplectic cocycles

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is a space with a flow
g_{t}
.
A
symplectic cocycle
for
g_{t}

(acting on
\mathbb{R}^{2m}
) is a map
\mathrm{C}\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{C}\mapsto\Sp(2m,\mathbb{R})
such that for all
q\in\mathcal{C}
and for all
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
we have the following:

•

\mathrm{C}(0,q)=\mathrm{Id}
and

•

\mathrm{C}(t+s,q)=\mathrm{C}(t,g_{s}q)\,\mathrm{C}(s,q)
.

The cocycle
\mathrm{C}
is
\log
–integrable
(with respect to a finite flow-invariant measure) if for all
t
the functions
q\mapsto\log\|\mathrm{C}(t,q)\|
and
q\mapsto\log\|\mathrm{C}(t,q)^{-1}\|
are integrable.
We will consider cocycles taking values in
\Sp(2m,\mathbb{Z})
.
For these, the function
q\mapsto\log\|\mathrm{C}(t,q)\|^{-1}
is bounded above and its integrability is implied by the finiteness of the invariant measure.
So it will suffice to show that
q\mapsto\log\|\mathrm{C}(t,q)\|
is integrable.

If the flow-invariant measure is ergodic, then Oseledets theorem
[
Ose68
,
BP13
]
applies.
It states that for almost every
q
in
\mathcal{C}
and every non-zero vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2m}
the limit

\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\frac{\|\mathrm{C}(t,q)\cdot v\|_{1}}{\|v\|_{1}}

exists and depends only on
v
(and not on
q
).
Moreover, the limit can achieve up to
2m
values, which, by symplecticity, have the form
\lambda_{1}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\lambda_{m}\geqslant-\lambda_{m}\geqslant\dots\geqslant-\lambda_{1}
.
These numbers are known as the
Lyapunov exponents
;
the set of numbers is the
Lyapunov spectrum
.
The spectrum is
simple
if all inequalities are strict.

7.7.
Konstevich–Zorich cocycles

By Lefschetz duality, the Hodge bundles
\mathrm{H}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}
can also be defined with fibres in
\mathrm{H}_{1}(S-Z(S);\mathbb{R})
or
\mathrm{H}_{1}(R-Z(R);\mathbb{R})
.
By a standard procedure of filling in the punctures, we also obtain vector bundles with fibres in
\mathrm{H}_{1}(S;\mathbb{R})
or
\mathrm{H}_{1}(R;\mathbb{R})
. By a slight abuse of terminology and notation, we also refer to them as Hodge bundles and denote them by
\mathrm{H}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}
.
In the sequel, we will only use this notation to refer to these versions with fibres in absolute homology of the (unpunctured) surfaces.

The (lift of the) diagonal flow acts on
{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}_{\mathrm{root}}\times\mathrm{H}_{1}(S,Z(S);\mathbb{R})
by
g_{t}((q,v),c)=(g_{t}(q,v),c)
.
Quotienting by the action of
\mathrm{Mod}(S,Z(S))
on both factors, we obtain the
Kontsevich–Zorich cocycle

\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{KZ}}
on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.

Suppose now that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
is quadratic.
As in
Section

7.3
we have an isomorphic copy of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
, called
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{root}}
lying in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{biroot}}
.
The pullback of
\mathrm{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
is (isomorphic to)
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}
.
As previously discussed, the pullback splits as a direct sum
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{+}\oplus\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{-}
called the plus and minus pieces.
The plus piece
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{+}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{H}_{\mathcal{C}}
while the minus piece
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{-}
is isomorphic to the tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{root}}
.
See
[
Tre13
, Sections 1 and 2]
.
The Kontsevich–Zorich cocycle
\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{KZ}}
on
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{biroot}}
pulls back and splits to give

•

the
plus cocycle

\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{KZ}}_{+}
, a copy of
\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{KZ}}
, and

•

the
minus cocycle

\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{KZ}}_{-}

on the plus and minus pieces, respectively.
Again, see
[
Tre13
, Sections 1 and 2]
.

The point of
Section

7.3
is to give a representation of
\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{-}
where the minus cocycle
\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{KZ}}_{-}
can be computed in terms of a basis of homology classes lying in
\mathrm{H}_{1}(R,\mathbb{Z})
.
We do this in
Section

7.11
.

7.8.
Log-integrability of the continuous cocycles

By
[
FM14
, Remark 31]
(which summarises consequences of
[
For02
, Lemma 2.1]
), the continuous Kontsevich–Zorich cocycle (over any
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
–orbit) is
\log
–integrable.
In particular, this implies the
\log
–integrability of
\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{KZ}}_{+}
and
\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{KZ}}_{-}
.

7.9.
Spanning sets for the plus piece

For each irreducible generalised permutation, there is a preferred set of cycles
\{c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
on
S-Z

[
Gut17
, Section 4.1]
.
These give a basis for
\mathrm{H}_{1}(S-Z;\mathbb{R})
and are (Lefschetz) dual to the rectilinear arcs in
4.26
, which belong to
\mathrm{H}_{1}(S,Z(S);\mathbb{R})
.
Lifting appropriately these also give a basis for
\mathrm{H}_{1}^{+}(R,Z(R);\mathbb{R})
.

As temporary notation, we take
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{KZ}}_{+}
to be the plus cocycle.
Suppose that
\pi
is a permutation.
Suppose that
\alpha
and
\beta
(in
\mathcal{A}
) are the rightmost top and bottom letters in
\pi
.
Breaking symmetry, suppose that
\xi\colon\pi\to\pi^{\prime}
is the Rauzy–Veech move in which
\alpha
wins.
Suppose that the cycles
c_{\alpha}
and
c_{\beta}
(for
\pi
) have non-zero algebraic intersection.
With respect to the plus spanning sets for
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
, we have

\mathrm{P}_{\xi}=\mathrm{Id}+E(\alpha,\beta)

(Here
E(\alpha,\beta)
is the matrix whose entries are zero except the
(\alpha,\beta)
–entry which is one.)
Similarly, if
c_{\alpha}
and
c_{\beta}
have zero algebraic intersection, then

\mathrm{P}_{\xi}=\mathrm{Id}-2E(\alpha,\alpha)-E(\alpha,\beta)

Recall that the linear change in width parameters in a finite Rauzy–Veech sequence
\delta
is expressed by the Rauzy matrix
E_{\delta}
(see
Equation

4.82
).

Lemma 7.10
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{KZ}}_{+}
is the plus co-cycle.
Then, for any finite Rauzy–Veech sequence
\delta
, we have the following:

\|\mathrm{P}_{\delta}\|_{1}\leqslant\|E_{\delta}\|_{1}

Proof.

Suppose that
C
is a matrix with real entries.
We define
|C|
to be the (non-negative) matrix whose coefficients are the absolute values of the coefficients of
C
.

For any Rauzy–Veech move
\delta
, the plus cocycle satisfies
|\mathrm{P}_{\delta}|=E_{\delta}
.
It follows that for any finite Rauzy–Veech sequence
\delta=\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\dotsc\delta_{k}

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{P}_{\delta}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\|\mathrm{P}_{\delta_{1}}\mathrm{P}_{\delta_{2}}\dotsc\mathrm{P}_{\delta_{k}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\||\mathrm{P}_{\delta_{1}}|\cdot|\mathrm{P}_{\delta_{2}}|\dotsc|\mathrm{P}_{\delta_{k}}|\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\|E_{\delta_{1}}E_{\delta_{2}}\dotsc E_{\delta_{k}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\|E_{\delta}\|_{1}\qed

7.11.
Spanning sets for the minus piece

We now use zippered rectangles to give an explicit spanning set for the minus piece.
Suppose that
\pi
is an irreducible labelled generalised permutation.
Suppose that
j=\sigma(i)
and
i<j
.
Let
g_{i}
and
g^{\prime}_{i}
be the two (oriented) lifts to
{\widetilde{q}}
of the rectilinear arc
\gamma_{i}
(as in
4.26
).
By construction, the involution
\iota
exchanges
g_{i}
and
g^{\prime}_{i}
preserving orientation.
Let
\alpha=\pi(i)
.
We now define the relative homology class

c_{\alpha}=[g_{i}]-[g^{\prime}_{i}]\in\mathrm{H}_{1}({\widetilde{S}},{\widetilde{Z}};\mathbb{R})

Applying
\iota
we find that
c_{\alpha}
lies in the minus piece
\mathrm{H}_{1}^{-}({\widetilde{S}},{\widetilde{Z}};\mathbb{R})
